ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e175	There is a proposal for a representative from Hallgeir Langeland, Snorre Serigstad Valen, Eirin Sund, Truls Wickholm, Marianne Marthinsen and Jette Christensen to investigate the republic as a form of government in Norway. This has ended in three proposals during the examination of the Committee on Control. The majority, consisting of the Central Party, the Christian People's Party, the Right, the Advance Party and the representatives of Kolberg and Nybakk from the Workers' Party, propose that the proposal be rejected. The minority – Jette Christensen, from the Workers’ Party, and SV – promote proposals to this effect, while the representative from the Left proposes that the proposal be annexed to the Minutes. I expect minorities to present their reasons for the proposals. What is the majority's assessment is that the Kingdom of Norway is a free, maritime and indivisible and inalienable empire, and that our constitutional monarchy is not a barrier to our constitutional democracy, where the Storing, as the supreme elected body, represents the power of the people. We are all aware that the king was deprived of political power at the 7th of June in 1905. The 7th of June and the referendum also agreed that the government would assume unlimited responsibility through the so-called countersignature of the king's decisions. This was subsequently confirmed by a separate constitutional amendment, § 31, on 15 August 1911. Members of the Government then had the right to reserve, i.e. not co-responsibility for the government's decision under the Constitution § 30. What's central is what the majority emphasizes, that our constitutional monarchy has acted in accordance with the constitutional rules that have existed since 1905. These are consistent with the view of the bulk population. For this reason, the constitutional monarchy has not been weakened. Therefore, to say that the abolition of the monarchy because it is not democratic, has no foundation in our governance, as enshrined in the Constitution. Furthermore, it is a prerequisite for our constitutional democracy that it must have a broad disintegration among people. In order for this to be cultivated in the future, all layers of the people must feel represented by the king and the queen exercising kingly power and symbolic headship. That's what's essential. Since 1905 the King and the Queen have acted according to our constitutional democracy and have acted in such a way that they embrace far and gather what is common understanding in Norwegian society. Thus has a broad disintegration about the royal role that one should fill. On this basis, on behalf of the majority, I recommend that the draft representative should not be adopted.

It's a very little radical proposal that lies in the Storting today. What is radical is to think that jobs and positions in Norway are to be inherited. We believe that positions, positions and positions in the Norwegian society are best assigned through people's trust through elections, not through inheritance. Yesterday we received a new discussion in the storage room about what Norwegian values are. There can be a lot of discussion, but I mean a continuous Norwegian value is that those who represent the people in positions of power are derived from the people. The reason why we've gone in to settle the world's most common democratic governance and see if it suits Norway is that we're of the opinion that jobs and positions are best distributed by measuring people's trust, not through inheritance. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e212	I would like to begin by thanking the case rapporteur for his introduction. I rely mainly on his assessments. I ask myself if it is a popular requirement in Norway to replace the monarchy with the Republic. Of course, there are some individuals who think this is a good idea, but in my experience it's a very, very small minority. I hardly remember anyone stopping me on the street and saying, "Now you at the Stortinget must soon have a republic introduced." Based on the people I'm talking to, I think the population can be divided into two main groups: those who are enthusiastic monarchies, and the more pragmatic ones who don't see the need to replace a system that works perfectly well. I define myself into the last group. In English they say, ‘If it isn’t broken, don’t fix it’. I don't think it makes sense to spend money and time figuring out something that we don't want or need. Our monarchy serves as a positive and collapsing symbol for the population. It may have a limited meaning in our everyday lives, but it is important when the nation is in crisis, in disasters, terrorist acts such as July 22, etc. I also believe that our monarchy has a positive impact on the marketing of our country. Our royal family has a significant travel activity and has contributed to putting Norway, Norwegian culture and Norwegian business on the map abroad. So they've done a very positive job. From a political point of view, the monarchy cannot be said to be a problem either. The real political power of the monarch has been gone for over 100 years, and our king has hardly been able to raise any political environments with his statements. It may not be all about the monarchy that causes considerable enthusiasm for the undersigned, but the scheme is basically very good and is a fine national symbol. It's a gathering institution, and it's positive in the marketing of our country. I am therefore in favour of not accepting the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e229	The form of Norway's rule is hereditary monarchy, and it implies that the formal state headship is held by a person who has inherited this position, and not given it by the people through elections. It was claimed in between that the king was given this position by election, because a choice was made more than 100 years ago, where less than half of the population could vote. In most other contexts, I don't think the Storting would have benefited from such a practice today. According to the Constitution, the king stands as head of state over us – the elected parliament – and even above the prime minister. Any position based on inherited rights is, of course, – and it is unnecessary to point out – an anachronism in a democratic society. The monarchy is a premise from a time when it was an authoritarian government and is therefore unsuitable as a democratic symbol. Last, but not least, a transition to the Republic is the natural end point of the democratisation that began in Norway in 1814, and which had been completed in many ways when the Stortinget deprived the king of the last remnants of formal power in the resolution of the Union, which several representatives have been in the past. It is not, however, that the Royal House today refrains from using political power on various occasions, and I will come back to that. The Republic is today the most common form of government in the world, and it is because it is the most natural form of government in the world. We – the proposers – want to change the Constitution, so that Norway too will have a head of state elected by the people, i.e. A president. There is a separate constitutional proposal on this, which we have discussed earlier, and which will also be discussed in the next period. Then I expect everyone to agree that within that time it is reasonable that there are alternatives and real assessments of what kind of governance should be put in place. Among the more central issues that should be addressed is: What power should a president have? How is the President to be elected? This proposal for a representative therefore proposes that the Stortinget should ask the Government to investigate different options for the Republic as a government in Norway and submit its assessments and proposals to the Stortinget in good time before the discussion – in the next period – of the constitutional proposal I have just mentioned. It has not been long since we dealt with previous proposals – which SV had promoted alone – on the introduction of republics as a form of government in Norway. Then there was no one in this room who was willing to defend the monarchy in principle. The argument that went back was that the proposal was too poorly investigated, that you didn't know what transition to the Republic would result from formalities, costs and what kind of presidential rule you should have. It is strange that the same representatives today will vote against our proposal for a proper investigation. And as the representative Christensen had in mind, it's a somewhat unusual practice. Usually, the Stortinget is very happy to have full clarity about what it is really about. This is a debate we need, because this is not just symbolism policies and markers. The King is given a place in our Constitution as sacred. It is contrary to the most fundamental democratic ideals of equivalency. In Christmas, there were probably a lot of people who saw the heir to the throne, Ingrid Alexandra, doing her first performance on TV. Ingrid Alexandra will grow up today without being able to engage politically. She will not be able to choose or choose religion. She will not be able to express herself freely about what she means. She will not be able to choose for herself what she will be. She will feel the pressure of an almost total collapse and millions of Northmen who expect her to be an ambassador and a representative of the country. As her father, she will also probably do it of course: yes, I have decided to adjourn, but it is right that I take over the throne interview at the NRK, not long after she becomes authoritative. But we all really want to know with ourselves that she has had no choice. That's not right. Heads of State must be elected and directly accountable to their people, and in modern democratic societies it is not that children from them are very small and throughout their lives should feel the pressure of millions of people and be told what they should feel, do and be. Many people very much disagree with our proposal, and that is good, because I want to debate, but then I miss the actual counter-arguments. I miss that we're going past the subjugating arguments. I've heard serious, respected politicians use arguments like, "Oh, are we supposed to have Carl I. Hagen as president?" That's not an argument for the monarchy. That's at best an argument to vote for someone other than Carl I. Hagen. That a politician could never be as unifying as the king is not an argument for the monarchy. That is an argument against democracy, and it is otherwise – and I regret the use of words – just customs and stuff. Of course, elected representatives can be, and are, gathering and national symbols – at good and bad: Kennedy, Gro, Gerhardsen, Kohl etc. It works so well today, the variation is the majority of the population has chosen to put on today, and that's quite right. We have a great royal family. They're good too, and they're good. I am not aiming at these appeals to criticism of the royal house that tabloid media practice among themselves, with guru appeals, angels, expensive bags that Mette-Marit has bought, etc. It's completely, completely on the side of the debate. But I aim that they largely refrain from politics – mostly. The Crown Prince begins to move into political waters with Young Global Leaders, IMF jobs, opening of aluminium works in authoritarian countries and much more. And the king gave clear notice to Trond Giske in 2008 about the future's requirements for the creed of the state at large. Then the king actively entered into our decision-making processes. It was accepted and treated as a trifle. But it's not. It's basically problematic. Mostly, the royal house keeps away from politics. There is something missing in this debate, and it is that some of the representatives who are today going to vote against this proposal come to this pulpit and hold their principal argument for the monarchy. It is not sustainable that we have a constitution that is not the majority of the population willing to defend in principle – because it works well today. In no other area of the Basic Law, such a weak argument, which is so weak in the principles upon which we have built this country, would be accepted. Is there anyone who would like to come up to the pulpit and make a flaming contribution to the king's sanctuary and indecentness? Is there anyone who wants to come up on this pulpit and make a flaming statement about the inheritance as a principle? Is there anyone here who wants to come up to the pulpit and tell me that the king is holy? Does the Progress Party mean it? Does the Right mean that? Does the Workers' Party mean it? The party of progress tends otherwise to be very happy to point out that things are not sacred, even those things that are most important to people. So far I haven't seen anyone who's willing to come up on this pulpit and do it, and I'm afraid we're not going to see it today either. However, in an informed society, in 2013, it should be expected that the burden of proof lies on those who believe that this metaphysically invented, wild, despicable, out-of-date, symbolic, practical problematic and conservative regime is the right way to organise one of the best and most democratic countries in the world. Shame, nostalgia, jokes about bad presidents and easy defecation by saying that it works well today, should no longer last. I had been delighted to debate, and I had hoped that the Storting could at least determine what it would be like, how we could take the step and become one of the countries that make up the great majority of the world that have no hereditary heads, but that debate we will not get because no one is willing to come up on this pulpit and defend the king's holiness and inviolability.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e340	The first speaker is the representative Per Olaf Lundteg, who is one of several rapporteurs on the subject.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e343	In the absence of the chairman of the committee, I have his role here today. First, I would like to refer to a letter from the Royal Ministry of Foreign Affairs, dated 15 March. It is from the State Council Eidsvoll Holmås to the Foreign Affairs Committee, with a copy to the Control Committee. Councils want to provide the Stortinget with up-to-date information on the administration and implementation of the 2011 budget by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The core issue is that the OECD has recommended that Norway change its practice when Norwegian aid for forest conservation in Brazil is considered to be official aid. This means that the funds can only be reported as official assistance when the funds are available to the recipient. However, Norway has so far reported this as official assistance in the year in which the funds are transferred to the bank account of Norway, and on the basis of the OECD recommendation, Norway has now adjusted its reporting. The size will be further briefed on the support for the rainforest management in Brazil in connection with the submission of the revised national budget since this year. So to the present case, as it is shown in the setting. I want to focus on the departments for which the committee chairman and I are the case rapporteurs, but I also want to take some general considerations. The Committee points out that the National Audit Office has audited the national accounts and the accounts of the administration of Svalbard. The review and control of the National Audit Office is part of the constitutional control system in the Norwegian Constitution. Similarly, the Stortinget is permitted to exercise control over the exercise of power, inter alia. through the annual audit of the accounts of the State. The 2011 national accounts show an oil-corrected deficit of 79,4 billion. kr versus 104.1 br. kr in 2010. The National Audit Office has issued 200 audit letters without comment and 24 audit letters with note in 2011, which are about the same level as in 2010. In Norway, central government continues to function well, but the National Audit Office continues to report serious weaknesses and deficiencies in several societally critical areas. It is particularly serious that the deficiencies are repeatedly pointed out, without them being corrected. This year, too, the National Audit Office reports on accounts that are not auditable. The Committee considers it extremely serious that accounts be made of such a quality that they cannot be audited. The Committee has noted that there are still activities that have not established a satisfactory risk management system as part of the target and performance management. The Committee points out that since 2003 the Financial Regulation has required the establishment of a system to assess and monitor the risk of non-achievement. Missing risk assessments may result in significant deviations from the target achievement being not identified, prevented or corrected. A recurring problem is the enforcement of the procurement rules, which must ensure the proper use of public funds and prevent maladministration and corruption. The national audit has for a number of years reported weaknesses and shortcomings in this area. The Committee notes that, although extensive measures have been put in place in recent years, they are not yet in place. There is still room for improvement, and the Committee asks the Riksrevision to follow up on this in the forthcoming review of the activities concerned. The Committee has noted that the Riksrevision reports on a significant amount of errors in the payment of benefits within welfare schemes and that this level exceeds the acceptable level. The Committee asks the Riksrevision to follow-up on this in the ongoing review. The Committee points out that both the National Audit Office and the Maritime Committee have focused on the Ministry's grant management in recent years. In the 2010 audit, the grant management of 13 ministries was checked. The National Audit Office had comments in nine cases. The Committee is concerned that the review for 2011 has shown that the measures taken have not provided the necessary improvements. In the winter of 2012, the Committee of the Regions carried out a study of the Ministry's grant management as set out below. Inner. 47 S for 2011-2012. The background was information in the media on irregular conditions of grant management in some ministries. The Committee’s studies showed that most of the data worked well. I want to underline it – most of it worked well. But many departments planned to improve their routines. For the treatment of Inst. 47 S decided to ask the National Audit Office to follow up the Ministry's grant management in its future audits. So on to the Ministry of Defence, which is one of the departments I am responsible for. There we point out that the Riksrevision has issued three final audit letters without notice and one final audit letter with a note to the Defence. The Committee further points out that the National Audit Office has significant observations relating to the accounts of the Defence Material Management, to the production of strength and operational activities, to acquisitions, to delays in investment projects and to the accounts of the new frigates. The Committee would point out that the Riksaudiert is serious about the fact that in 2011, almost half of the Armed Forces’ operating acquisitions, nearly three years after the introduction of the FIF 2.0, did not follow the main process of the system, since the main process is to be used for acquisitions in order to capture the use of budgetary resources in the commitment accounts, thereby improving the ability of the defence to exercise effective financial management. The Committee notes that the main process was followed in 80 pst. of the cases in May 2012. The Committee, like the Riksrevision, assumes that in the management dialogue with the Defence Department this relationship is specifically followed up. Furthermore, the Committee notes that the Ministry of Defence continues that the defence has competence challenges and that in several areas there is a need for better and more appropriate complex competences. The Committee expects that the competence situation in the defence as a whole will be addressed in a separate, forthcoming paper. The Committee will note that the Riksrevision concludes that measures taken to address weaknesses in procurement processes have not had an expected effect, but that the Ministry of Defence, for example, has had an impact. works to recruit suitably qualified personnel. The Committee notes that the Riksrevision is serious about the fact that nearly half of the 42 medium-sized investment projects audited were delayed by one year or more compared to the original plans and that eight of them were delayed by at least three years. According to the National Audit Office, these delays can largely stem from the resource situation of the Defence Logistics Organisation (FLO). The Committee would point out that the Stortinge has previously criticised major delays in defence investment projects on several occasions. The Committee notes that through the governance dialogue with the Defence Department, the Ministry of Defence will provide clear priorities related to the investment portfolio. The Committee notes that the Riksrevision is serious about the challenges of crewing technical staff on board the frigates. The frigates are complex systems that impose high standards of competence. The Committee notes that the Ministry of Defence admits that it will take some time before the actions undertaken within parts of the FLO, which will support the operational structure of the Maritime Defence Agency, will have the necessary effect. The Committee would stress the importance of prioritising the Ministry of Defence to ensure high operational capacity for the frigates. The Committee takes note of the statement by the Ministry that the sailing activity for 2011 was at the level planned for the end of the long term 2009-2012. As regards the Ministry of Knowledge, the Committee will refer to the audit and control of the Ministry and its underlying activities by the National Audit Office. The Committee notes that 43 final audit letters have been issued without comments and six with comments on the University of Oslo, Norway's Higher Music School, Norway's School of Sport, the School of Architecture and Design in Oslo, the University of Volda and the Centre for Internationalisation of Education respectively. The Committee points out that, in its letter of 31 January 2013 to the Ministry, it raised questions of non-compliance with the rules for the remuneration of scientific staff in the university and higher education sectors, the UH sector, and breaches of the rules on public acquisitions. The Council of State replied in a letter dated 8 February 2013. The letter is attached to the setting. Furthermore, the Committee has noted that more than one establishment in the higher education sector still does not comply with the rules on wages and salaries of scientific staff. The Ministry of Knowledge has stated in its reply to the Committee that several measures have been put in place to strengthen and clarify the role and obligations of the Board of Directors, in order to ensure that institutions are in a position to avoid breaches of legislation. Conditions are included at the etat management meetings with the institutions concerned and in the letters of allocation for 2012. This will also be followed up in the 2013 governance dialogue. The Committee expects the institutions concerned to follow up, so that the matters referred to are addressed. Furthermore, the Committee noted that the Riksrevision also pointed out for 2011 that a number of scientific staff have been involved in beekeeping and private business without the existence of procedures to ensure sufficient transparency around these sites. The Committee considers it desirable for scientific staff in the sector to be engaged in business and social activities outside the sector, but stresses the importance of ensuring that beekeeping and participation do not conflict with the primary position, so that the confidence in the independence of scientific staff is weakened. Transparency on these issues is important. The Committee therefore asks the Ministry to ensure compliance with the legislation so that there are no conflicts of role and stability. The Committee has noted that an overarching theme for the establishments which have received audit letters with comments is a lack of compliance with the acquisition legislation. This applies, as mentioned above, to five out of six establishments. The Ministry has stated in the letter of reply that they take a serious view of the facts that the Riksaudition has revealed both in terms of compliance with the legislation and the financial statements themselves for 2011. All institutions that have received significant comments on the annual accounts for 2011 have been followed up by the Ministry at various meetings in spring and autumn 2012 to ensure that the necessary measures have been taken. The Committee has noted that in 2013 the Ministry will have meetings of the Management Board with the activities that have received significant comments on the annual accounts for 2011 and that the Ministry will then assess the need for further follow-up measures to the institutions concerned. As regards the Ministry of the Environment, the Committee points out that the Riksrevision has issued seven final audit letters without comment and two final audit letters with note. This concerns the Directorate of Nature Management and the Norwegian Cultural Memory Fund. The Committee further points out that the Riksrevision has significant observations relating to the accounts of grants for preventive and conflict mitigation measures in the management of predators and Norwegian Cultural Memory Fund. The Committee notes that the Riksrevision considers it critical that the Ministry has not reviewed an evaluation of the system of grants for preventive and conflict mitigation measures in the management of wild game. The Committee has also noted that the audit has shown that the reporting on the grant scheme, obtained by the Directorate for Nature Management, is deficient and that funds have been allocated to measures that are considered not to have a direct loss reduction effect. The Committee finds it extremely unfortunate that funds are allocated to measures that are considered not to have a direct loss reduction effect. The Committee notes that the Ministry has informed the National Audit Office that they will review the legislation in order to ensure that the grant scheme is practised as equally as possible. The Committee would point out that, under the mandate of the Ministry, the Directorate for Nature Management has laid down new rules for grants for preventive and conflict mitigation measures in the management of wild game in force from 1 January of the current year to 2013. The Committee points out that the Riksrevision is critical of the fact that the Ministry of the Environment has not satisfactorily followed up established conditions for the grant management of the Cultural Memory Fund. The Committee would point out that the rationale for the establishment of the Cultural Memory Fund was the need to strengthen efforts to ensure the protection of cultural memories and environments. Like the National Audit Office, the Committee takes a serious view that grants linked to commitments resulting from the poor follow-up of the Cultural Memory Fund may have contributed to the non-implementation as foreseen of projects for the protection of valuable cultural memories or environments. The Committee notes that the Cultural Memory Fund has informed the Ministry of the Environment that it would clean up in 2012 all older, open commitments. The Committee therefore considers that this has been done. The Committee notes that the ongoing review will follow-up on the issue of the management of grant funds. With regard to the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade, the Committee refers to the audit and control of the Ministry and its underlying activities by the National Audit Office. The Committee has noted that eight final audit letters have been issued without comments and two audit letters with comments. This concerns Norwegian Romsenter and the Export Credit Guarantee Institute, GIEK. The Committee points out that the questions raised by letter of 31 January 2013 to the Ministry concerning the Altinn, GIEK and Norsk Romsenter were raised in its letter of 31 January 2013. The Council of State replied by letter of 11 February 2013. The letters are attached to the setting. The Committee considers it extremely serious that there has been an increase in the number of adverse events in the region in the last few years, so that operational interruptions and critical error situations have occurred. It is also very serious that the problems of the existing solution have hampered the deployment of new services and created uncertainty about Altin to address the volumes needed by Nav in the solution. According to the Ministry’s reply to the Committee, the current Altinn platform is to be phased out during the summer of 2013. Together with the work on a new tax solution, to be implemented during the first half of 2013, the Ministry expects new ALTIN solution both to be robust and to have sufficient capacity to receive new services and service owners, including several Nav services. The Committee expects the Ministry to follow up and assess the need for measures to ensure that the current ALTIN solution also has the operational security necessary for the examination of the declarations in spring 2013. The Committee points out that, in several areas, GIEK has had poor governance of its own activities, including: cost control and budgetary management. The Ministry states in the letter of reply that in meeting GIEK they have taken up the noted comments from the Riksrevision and discussed the necessary follow-up actions. The Committee has noted that the Ministry has asked for priority in the 2013 letter of allocation and that the Ministry will follow up this work in the ordinary governance dialogue with GIEK.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e505	I should just like to make a few comments on aid, i.e. development aid from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, also in view of the letter sent by the Minister for Development, dated 15 March, which was also presented by the Vice-President of the Committee. This concerns the 2011 budget. It is such that the OECD DAC Development Assistance Committee, the Committee that approves each year what can be accepted as official development assistance, official development assistance, defines not only what kind of measures and projects can be approved as official development assistance, but also which countries can be defined and approved as recipients. This means that only aid to countries eligible for eligibility as beneficiaries and the projects defined as assistance by this Committee can count on when the aid's share of gross national income is to be approved. This also applies in Norway. This sometimes creates complications. It is not rare that Norwegians have also wanted to provide assistance to countries that are not among the OECD countries that accept Norwegian aid. During the previous period, Norway chose to add the so-called rainforest money to Brazil and elsewhere to the aid budget. It was a fairly thorough and lengthy discussion – and debate – about this, because it was not given that these rain forest resources should be part of the one percent we spend on Norwegian aid. The OECD now recommends that Norway change its practice when Norwegian support for forest conservation in Brazil is considered official assistance. I have not understood that the OECD DAC questions whether rainforest money should be part of Norwegian aid, but that it is about when Norwegian aid for forest conservation in Brazil is to be regarded as official Norwegian aid. This means that the funds can only be reported as official when they are available to recipients. I expect that the debate and discussions between the Norwegian Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the DAC Committee in the OECD will work reasonably well, taking into account that Erik Solheim has a rather central place in that system at the moment. After that, the 2011 aid budget is 26.7 billion. kr, i.e. 0.96 pst. of gross national income. This is quite technical, but it is important to follow international rules of play. It is a goal for the government and for the Workers' Party that we reach 1 pst. annual gross national income in aid to combat poverty, reaching the United Nations’ Millennium Development Goals and in order to improve the environment. At the same time, it is important that we also have a continuous debate on the content of the same assistance – this in order to ensure the Millennium Goals, in order to ensure a development policy that works, in order to strengthen democracy and democratic movements, in order to strengthen the situation of women and to provide an understanding that women's work actually contributes to growth and development, and indeed creates values. This is a broad consensus on assistance, with some exceptions – of course – also in the Chamber here. The National Audit Office has revised nine foreign stations and has not identified significant errors or deficiencies. I think that's something that should be emphasized. It is reassuring and shows that we have good management also in our outings when it comes to assistance, as Norad went into the regular outings several years ago. The Committee has attached some comments to the report of the National Audit Office on the management of assistance to Zambia. Zambia is a country far south of Africa. The Committee says that we are aware of the problems that lie there, including: with corruption – this is not the first time corruption has been detected in some aid projects in Zambia. Let me add that there is an overall committee that emphasizes that in the area of corruption we have zero tolerance as the guide for our aid policy. I want to add one thing. Sometimes assistance to some countries becomes static. Zambia was one of the first countries to become the main co-operation country for Norwegian development assistance. It's starting to be a few years ago. Zambia was an important frontline country under the apartheid regime in South Africa, and at that time Norway provided very much assistance to refugees from South Africa who were in Zambia. Now it is actually quite many years since that regime disappeared, and it may be other countries in Africa that deserve more assistance than a country like Zambia. This applies, inter alia, to: countries in West Africa, e.g. Liberia. It's a different discussion, but I chose to mention it because I think we might have a tendency to continue in the same track and have the same projects year after year. But the world is changing, so are some developing countries.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e570	The Committee has examined the Riksrevision’s inquiry into the management of aquaculture. One has highlighted three superior problem settings. One was the extent to which the evolution and status of the aquaculture industry in Norway are in line with the national target seen by the Storting to be resilient and environmentally compatible. The second was the extent to which national targets for sustainable aquaculture are achieved through drug use. And the third was whether the governance of the authorities is sufficient to ensure that the development of the aquaculture industry is sustainable. The marine industry in Norway has grown significantly over several decades. It is an important industry and it employs many, but the National Audit Office's investigation shows that the growth has had its consequences. Especially in areas with large and dense farming, the environmental consequences have been great. The main objective of the major agricultural policy is to ensure an environmental and sustainable aquaculture industry, while facilitating further growth and development in the industry. The relevance of the environment shall be a fundamental premise for further development and growth. There is a joint committee that looks very seriously at some of the Riksrevision findings. Many places are not such that the industry has had an environmentally friendly growth. In the area of fastening of farming, increased incidence of fish diseases and lice has been found and the incorporation of escaped farmed salmon is significant. The wildax population has been reduced. In 2010 it lost 47 million salmonids. Many of the losses are caused by disease, and the disease situation has not changed since 2000, we could read in the Riksrevision report. The study showed that there is a lack of knowledge when it relates to regional effects of emissions from the farming industry. Indicators are missing that can tell the extent to which management reaches the target of targeted and sustainable farming; supervision has too many regulatory breaks; similar cases may have different outcomes in an application process. The National Audit Office points in many problematic areas. The size would like to welcome both the wild and farmed industries, and we would like to make it possible for both industries to take account of each other and to be in common. Therefore, we also arranged a hearing in Stortinget to facilitate Norwegian policies for both the wild and farming industries. In the hearing, Mr Lisbeth Berg-Hansen and Minister for the Environment, Mr Bård Vegar Solhjell, Mr Lakseelver were represented, Mr Jager and Mr Fiskers were represented, the Norwegian Environmental Protection Association was there, and Mr Marine Harvest AS, Mr Osland Havbruk AS, Mr Cermaq ASA, the Land Association of Fisheries and the Marine Research Institute, Mr Sintef, the Veterinary Institute and the Norwegian Institute for Natural Research were all in the audience. It was a useful hearing that the committee got a lot out of. The main cause of the hearing was to highlight different problem options from the different interests associated with strengthening the management of aquaculture and to ensure both environmental resilience and the potential for future growth. As I said, it was a useful hearing, which was important for our treatment of this matter. The Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs is not the only ones who respond to the situation; the Ministry of Fisheries and the Coastal Department also believes that the situation was far from ideal. But it may seem that the results of the National Audit Office's report did not come as a surprise to the Ministry, because the environmental challenges raised in the National Audit Office's report were the same as the Ministry itself has pointed out both before and after the National Audit Office's findings were presented. The fact that the ministry has undertaken several measures to remedy the situation will not only correct the environmental problems but also make the use of the seas more efficient and limit economic losses for the industry. That's right there. In the reply from the Ministry of Fisheries and the Coastal Department of the Riksrevision, they say that ensuring environmental adaptation of aquaculture, reducing loss and disease, strengthening of fish health, reducing escape, resilient feed, a strengthened tool and a transparent and holistic management of the industry are priorities in the ministry’s work. That's good. Several measures have been taken by the government. They have seen 29 out of 32 measures taken by the government’s strategy for an environmentally and sustainable aquaculture industry. In the wake of the news about seafood coming very soon, there is a long-term discussion of the principle of zoning. The Committee believes it is positive that the Ministry is committed to nationally and internationally to wild marine resources. As rapporteur, I feel that the Committee and the Ministry share their concerns about the current situation, and that the Ministry has done a great deal and has announced more measures to remedy it. It is therefore in everyone's interest that the Committee calls on the National Audit Office to follow up the Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs' work on improving the management of marine fisheries in its future audits to examine whether the changes that have been reflected have been both implemented and have had the effect that the Ministry wants and that the Stortinget also wants them to have.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e593	The marine industry has been growing significantly over several years and today contributes to high value creation, many jobs and high export incomes for our country. One of the main objectives of aquaculture policy is to ensure an environmental and sustainable aquaculture industry, while facilitating further growth and development in industry. The overall vision of fisheries policy is that the values of the sea represent the future of Norway, while on several occasions the Storting has stated that environmental considerations should be a fundamental premise for further development and growth. ‘The aquaculture industry has had significant environmental challenges through, inter alia, high escape rates, salmon and extensive losses due to disease.’ This is how National Auditor Jørgen Kosmo expressed himself when the report we are discussing today was handed over to Stortinget last year. He also said that these and other environmental challenges are of a scale that requires strengthening the management of aquaculture in order to ensure environmental sustainability and the potential for future growth in industry. After reviewing the report, it is not difficult to agree to this. A unanimous committee welcomes the fact that environmental considerations have been given increasing importance in decisions on production growth taken in cooperation between the Ministry of Fisheries and the Ministry of the Environment. At the same time, a unanimous committee takes a very serious view of some of the findings of the National Audit Office’s inquiry into the management of marine life. I refer in this connection to the large loss of fish due to disease, as well as the high incidences of salmontus. The disease situation has not improved since the year 2000 and the large losses have resulted in significant economic losses for the industry and inefficient use of sea areas. According to the study, the incidence of salmonids is still at an excessive level along large sections of our coast. This leads primarily to a reduction in fish welfare, but for wild fish and sea trout survival is also reduced. Another problem is that the increasing proportion of escaped farmed fish among wild fish can both lead to the spread of disease and will change the genetic material of the wild species over time. Unfortunately, the objective of reducing impacts threatening the genetic diversity of salmon to a non-harmful level by 2010 has not been achieved. This is serious. The marine industry also contributes to the release of large amounts of nutrient salts, organic matter and chemicals. The impact of total emissions is not clear today, but it is beyond doubt that chemicals used in salmon dissipation treatment cause damage to nature. Another challenge for the industry is the use of large quantities of wild fish for feeding. The pressure on some species has been great, and it is therefore very important that we ensure that all the wild resources contained in fish feed are from sustainable fishing. The Riksrevision study concludes that the aim of Stortingen for the industry to have an environmentally friendly growth is unfortunately not achieved in many places. The study also points out that current use of instruments is not adapted to the environmental challenges. The need for more efficient and predictable management of the aquaculture industry to ensure overall and more consistent regulation and treatment of installations is clearly present. It is positive that most of the government’s strategy for an environmentally sustainable aquaculture industry has been implemented, but this is obviously not enough. The Progress Party therefore welcomes the fact that a unanimous committee concludes that further action needs to be considered if the effects of the measures already in place are not soon to be seen more clearly. It is also essential that we put in place the necessary indicators which make it measurable whether the figures for loss, disease, salmonids and the impact of escaped farmed fish are low enough. A unanimous committee considers that the ministry must give priority to the establishment of quality standards, indicators and limit values as soon as possible. It is also positive that the industry has set up its own catch fund, so that you can be paid if you catch escaped farmed fish in the rivers. Rumid farmed fish in rivers are environmental pollution, and the polluter pays principle must also apply here. It is therefore important to intensify work on developing a system that can trace the fish to the site from which it has been evacuated. The overall assessment by the national audit of the environmental challenges of the aquaculture industry is serious and requires follow-up. In the Committee’s hearing on 3 December 2012, we received many constructive and useful insights into how to solve today’s challenges to industry. The Progress Party therefore looks forward to the consideration of the announced paper on the maritime food industry. It's during the treatment of this we're going to outsource the course for the future aquaculture industry in Norway. When we see the broad consensus that has been in this matter in the control committee, the Progress Party is optimistic. The Progress Party hopes that we can find broadly anchored solutions that will ensure further development and growth for this important industry for Norway, within an environmental and sustainable framework.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e628	First, the National Audit Office’s survey of the management of the aquaculture sector shows a young industry that has developed rapidly. Marine farming, the whitefish and pelagic sector, what we like to mention today with the common name ‘seafood industry’, has become our second most important export industry and is referred to as one of the three national knowledge clusters, alongside energy and maritime industry. So we're talking about one of the fields where in Norway we've taken the natural benefits we have, and developed them to be among the leading environments in the world. The National Audit Office’s report highlights a number of challenges both for industry and for others affected by it. It is therefore reassuring that the challenges pointed out by the National Audit Office are also addressed in the administration and in the industry itself. The industry on its side can, since the audit took place, show great improvements for many of the challenges. The management is challenged in the brief report that good governance requires clear objectives, which in turn requires clear targets. It is not satisfactory that you cannot point this out, but it is certainly reassuring that you can point to the work with a message to get this done. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e696	The marine industry has evolved into one of the most important areas of value creation, export income and employment in Norwegian business. Between 2000 and 2010, the total production of farmed fish and shellfish in Norway was more than doubled, from about one to two. 500000 tonnes to over 1 million tonnes per year. The sales value of aquaculture production has passed 30 billion. kr per year. We have reason to be proud of an industry that puts Norway on the world map, with very high-quality seafood products. As a representative of one of the largest aquaculture communities in Norway, I would also like to stress the importance of the aquaculture industry for value creation and employment in rural areas. Operation of supermarkets and processing of raw materials from the aquaculture industry create thousands of jobs in Norwegian district municipalities. As seen, this industry greatly contributes to maintaining a scattered settlement along our coast. At a time when other primary industries are under pressure, with a decrease in the number of employed and poor profitability, the importance of the aquaculture industry is increasing to ensure that it will continue to shine from the windows of small rural communities along the Norwegian coast. However, it is a fact that the aquaculture industry has gradually grown to a size and total production volume causing significant growth pain. With regard to both the escape of farmed fish, disease and parasites in farms and the release of organic material and chemicals, the industry faces significant challenges. In such a situation, it is all the more important that the industry itself and the authorities responsible for the management of aquaculture work together to ensure sustainable development for Norwegian aquaculture. No one, and at least the industry itself, has benefited from a development that endangers coastal ecosystems, and which in the long run harms the good reputation of Norwegian seafood in the world. The National Audit Office has carried out a larger survey to find answers as to whether the management of aquaculture is doing its job to ensure sustainable development in the aquaculture industry. One of the key questions underpinning the study was the extent to which the use of the instruments of the authorities has been implemented and a means of ensuring that the national sustainable aquaculture targets are met. Unfortunately, the report of the National Audit Office shows that the overall objective of sustainable growth and the development of the aquaculture industry, which has been underpinned by an overall increase, cannot be said to have been achieved by the current administrative regime. The conclusions of the Riksrevision are quite unambiguous at this point, and I quote from page 8 of the Committee's position: ‘The Riks revision is of the opinion that the environmental challenges in the aquaculture industry have become so extensive that the industry cannot be said to have had sufficient environmentally adapted growth, as the Storting has foreseen. The environmental challenges in industry are, according to the National Audit Office, an extent that will require significant changes in the management of aquaculture and the way in which the aquaculture industry is regulated.» More clearly, it can't be said! The National Audit Board bases its conclusion on a number of weaknesses and unfortunate aspects of Norwegian aquaculture management. I will briefly mention some of the most important. In St.meld. No 19 for 2004–2005, on marine economic development, it was suggested that an overall strategy for making the use of available land by the aquaculture industry more effective. However, the granting of new and amended authorisations for the breeding of salmonids has taken place without the existence of an overall strategy for making use of the area more effective. In the application process of authorisations for farming, evaluations are mainly carried out on conditions related to the individual site and not on the overall burden of several farms in a larger area. The lack of such an overall strategy is becoming all the more serious given that it is precisely in areas with many farms that the problems of salmon, diseases and escaped farmed salmon are greatest. The National Audit Office further points out that the Ministry of Fisheries and the Coastal Ministry of Fisheries has developed little indicators which may say something about whether the management achieves the objectives of a sustainable and environmentally adapted aquaculture industry. This applies, for example: maximum levels of loss, disease, salmonids and genetic incorporation of escaped farmed fish. It must also be mentioned that the studies used to identify the impact of water quality and bottom conditions in farms are not adapted to the size of today’s facilities. This leads to a serious lack of knowledge about the regional impact of different types of emissions from the breeding industry. As a result, there is a risk that measurements of the environmental status of farms will simply be misleading. A third aspect that is also serious is the fact that the Riksrevision has identified major regional differences in the way in which supervision of the industry is carried out. This applies, inter alia, to: the extent to which the Food and Fisheries Directorate applies sanctions and enforcement measures in relation to supervision. Here the numbers vary between 2 and 24 pst. in the regions of the Fisheries Directorate and between 9 and 17 pst. in the regions of the Food Authority. What these numbers show is both alarming and alarming. In reality, there is no similarity between the law for operators in the Norwegian aquaculture industry — it is actually the location of the facilities that determines the likelihood of coercive action being taken. At the same time as the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee has been working on the situation of the Storting in this matter, the Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs has drawn up a proposal for a regulation on the award of green salmon concessions. Out of a total of 45 rights, 35 shall be granted in closed bidding rounds requiring the redemption of old concessions. In this bidding round, there is a requirement that the applicant undertakes to implement solutions that reduce environmental problems compared to those in commercial use today. When it became known that the Ministry would announce the award of so-called green salmon concessions, the Christian People's Party called for clear and specific criteria on which environmental measures would be required before an applicant could be granted a green concession. It would be natural for the regulation to mention concrete measures to reduce the number of escaped fish and to reduce disease and salmonids in the plant. One measure in question may be the implementation of closed plants in the first part of the production line for farmed fish. A month ago, a mini-seminar was held at Stortinget where various economic operators presented new and innovative solutions for closed farms. This is mainly plant for sebumfish and smolt. They will provide almost total protection against disease infection and salmonidosis and will minimise the risk of escape. One of the economic operators at the seminar could indicate that they had carried out a full-scale test of their concept over several months, without any recorded dead fish in the facility. What we heard and saw during the seminar showed that closed farms have reached a technological maturity level that makes it fully possible to implement the solutions in the first part of the production process. The clear signal from those who presented their solutions was that needed political action is now needed to ensure that the new technology becomes commercially profitable for those who have taken the work and the costs of developing it – or to say so in a slightly different way: where the industry tends to weigh disadvantages over benefits and is struggling for new investment, the ministry and the administration must make demands that make new technology a part of the solution. In this respect, it should also be expected that the aquaculture industry will take greater account of the high performance potential of closed installations. Loss factors such as diseases, salmonids and escape will be significantly reduced in such installations, with the positive economic repercussions that it will produce. It was therefore disappointing to record just a lack of concrete demands for action when the Ministry sent out the green salmon concession award regulation at the hearing on 4 March this year. For the closed concession round and the permits in Troms and Finnmark, only two general requirements were imposed: either that the new concessions would reduce the risk of the impact of wild salmonids as a result of escape or that there would be no more than 0,25 female lice per fish in the plant. With regard to the last requirement, this is not even a status quo in the fight against salmonids. As of today, the requirement is 0,5 female per fish in farms. If such a concession is withdrawn and replaced by two new ones where the requirement is 0.25 lux per fish, one is exactly the same distance. The only thing that has happened then is spreading the risk of infection in two locations instead of one. It is regrettable that the Ministry of Fisheries and the Coast abused the chance they had to impose specific requirements on more environmentally efficient and sustainable aquaculture in the context of the so-called green salmon concessions. In view of the sharp criticism of the Riksrevision's report, and the clear leadership of the Stortinge on sustainable development in the industry, the department's lack of action becomes almost incomprehensible. Like the other parties, the Christian People's Party will stress that we are looking very seriously at some of the findings of the National Audit Office's survey of marine management. However, we choose to take a positive view that the department seems to take seriously the need for a new spatial structure. The Christian People's Party is looking forward to a thorough discussion of this issue when the announced news of maritime nutrition is presented. It is also positive that the Ministry recognises the issues raised by the Riksrevision and that 29 out of 32 of those measures have been initiated or implemented in the Government's strategy for an environmentally sustainable aquaculture industry. In addition to greater clarity in the requirements for sustainable development measures, the Christian People’s Party will point in particular to the need for clear indicators to measure the level of disease, salmonids and the impact of escaped farmed fish. Adequate level of knowledge in these areas is crucial to the deployment of effective and targeted measures when the limit values are exceeded. Finally, we will stress the need for a uniform system of supervision that eliminates regional differences in the use of sanctions and enforcement measures against industry. The Christian People's Party is aware that proposals for amendments to the Aquaculture Act to ensure equal treatment and efficiency in management have been consulted. It's positive. We believe, however, that that is not enough. The Ministry needs to provide several measures to ensure equal treatment of industry actors regardless of location.

The Industry Committee has just held a seminar on closed installations. There's a lot of exciting happening in this area. There's a lot of different ways to do it -- everything from tight vessels that need to be attached to the bottom, to tarpaulins. But what's common denominator is that none of these are currently ready to be used commercially in the ordinary salmon production. Several of them are now initiating trials, and in the first instance it is linked to smolt and cephalopods. Today, there is an exceptional provision in which you can actually be granted a concession to breed cephalopods and smolts. It's increased from 250 grams to 1,000 grams, i.e. 1 kg. But this is also a production that can be done on land today. Several are planning to start the production of fish up to 1 kg on land. Are they in the plant’s facilities, or is the total quantity to be produced in the country as a whole? He uses this as a pure fillet, produces it on land, slaughters it and sells it out. These will meet major challenges: Does this come under a food-fish concession or as a seed-fish concession? When you produce the fillet of mackerel, very much of that silage is returned again just to produce feed for, among other things. the breeding industry. We need to do research on this, so that we can do it even better, make it even more profitable and more sustainable. It is important that we do these things in consultation with the industry and do not care about the industry. It's important that we come forward. This is an industry for growth. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e935	Today, we are dealing with Document 3:2, the National Audit Office's control of the administration of state companies for 2011. So this is an annual report that, like Document 1, is an important part of the National Audit Office's tasks. This report covers many companies, but four so-called extended controls have been carried out. I'm essentially concentrating on these. The objective of the company control is to assess whether the management of the State’s interests is consistent with the decisions and assumptions of the Storting. This survey includes 45 whole-time and 32 shared companies. The management of the state ownership of the companies is subject to a total of 10 departments. The report covers a five-year period from 2007 to 2011. The companies are grouped into categories based on the characteristics of the companies and the breakdown is clarified with the owners. Some are companies with business objectives; others are companies with sectorial tasks and objectives. It has been demonstrated in the report that the effectiveness and financial performance of State undertakings should be better monitored. That's one of the main conclusions. In my opinion, follow-up is central to state ownership. There are several departments and businesses that should better deal with this and also have clearer returns targets. The State shall be a demanding owner. After all, the State manages all of this on behalf of taxpayers, and then it is important to be a clear and demanding owner. For several of the companies with return targets, the average return over the period 2007-2011 was significantly lower than the return target. The NSB and Posten, for example, had an average return below five percentage points below the yield target. Let me add that even if companies have special tasks, which the government parties have a comment on here, they should also set returns targets for these companies, and it is important that the follow-up ensures that the yield target can be maintained. Effective operation is also part of the objective of state ownership, and so is the case in companies that do not pursue purely business returns. In the four extended controls carried out, the main findings are as follows: in several health establishments, waiting time is incorrectly recorded. This leads to uncertainty for both hospitals and patients. It is also this time a critical mention of the conversion process at Oslo University Hospital. The owner control is described here as weak. Reference is made to the fact that Health South-East accepted a very high implementation risk in this migration process. Health South-East knew that Oslo University Hospital in 2009 and 2010 had repeatedly reported on the critical risk associated with the complexity of the conversion mission they were assigned. The National Audit Office points out that neither Health South-East nor the Ministry adapted its management and took the necessary corrective action at an early enough time. These issues have been dealt with repeatedly by Stortinget, in other control contexts, and under health ministers other than the current one. So to the Ministry of Culture: the productivity of NRK television production has been significantly reduced after 2008, while the proportion of programmes sent with high production costs has decreased. Up to now, the Ministry of Culture has not obtained information suitable for assessing the effectiveness of this operation in relation to costs, following the National Audit Office's assessment. So to the Ministry of Knowledge: several student relationships have not adapted their operations to their financial frameworks and opportunities. Several of the interactions also have weaknesses in economic governance. It has been demonstrated for many years that the quality of health care reporting is poor and that incorrect recording of waiting time has consequences for individual patient’s ability to plan treatment and choose a treatment site. There is consensus in the Committee that this is a serious matter, but the government fraction underlines in its comments, like the ministry, that it is not possible to detect deviations without entering each individual journal. Nevertheless, there is agreement that this field needs to be improved, and a unanimous committee emphasises that work on ensuring proper registration practices must be of high priority. The majority of the committee, i.e. the opposition parties on Stortinget, is also seriously concerned that it has been revealed that Health South-East accepted a very high implementation risk, as I have already mentioned, before the conversion task was given to Oslo University Hospital, OYS. The Committee also agrees that the OYS migration should now be closely monitored. With regard to the NRC, the Committee is unanimous in its view that the operation should be efficient, and we stress the importance of the Ministry of Culture assessing the effectiveness of the operation of the NRC. I have to add, on my own account, that this is clearly a somewhat strange task for the Ministry of Culture, but they manage a large and important – including economic – institution, and then they should take this part of the criticism of the National Audit Office seriously. In the field of the economic governance of student co-operation, an overall committee also points to the need to further strengthen the supervision and follow-up of relations and asks the Ministry of Knowledge to pay particular attention to this field in the future. A theme that is not part of the National Audit Office's investigations, essentially, at least, but which is found to be serious by both a majority and a minority, is that the management salaries of the 42 companies continue to increase significantly more than the overall management wage growth of society. But I have to add that the majority parties also record that a number of measures have been implemented to improve the management of wage growth. Representatives of the Government Parties may comment on their own comments, pointing out that the departments concerned at forthcoming general meetings promote, if necessary, the introduction of the Protocol, so that the Management Board is entrusted with the task of reviewing the overall financial compensation for the Administrative Manager. There's a lot of exciting happening around. In fact, it is rare to experience that a staging message is received as well in the field as the ICT message has been. As rapporteur of the case, I have met various actors involved in the message, attended current conferences and listened to the hearing bodies that Stortinget met in connection with the processing of the message. It is very reassuring that all stakeholders agree on the challenge picture drawn up in the message and that all subscribe to the key concepts outlined. Norwegian people are used to communicating and retrieving online information. The ICT strategy will give us new opportunities for both: good health information, transparency in personal data and the possibility to engage in dialogue with different parts of the health, care and care sectors. Not least, in a country like ours, there will be a lot to get into use, for example. telemedicine solutions in collaboration between treatment providers and in interaction between treatment and patients/users. The ICT message describes several important strategic approaches. The many and scattered patient journal systems we have need to be reduced, and patient/user health information needs to be coordinated in an integrated journal system. Health information must follow the patient/user wherever treatment and other health care are to be provided — regardless of where in the system or geography this is taking place. The message provides for better structuring of patient data. It is important, too much of the patient information in journals, referrals and epicrisis is free text. Therefore, we need a common terminology and a clinical coding of data, so that systems support the processing and sharing of information across healthcare levels. Information in the patient record shall be capable of being handled automatically when, for example: shall be submitted to the health records and to the planned core file. Privacy and patient safety are very important for further work on the ICT strategy. All systems dealing with information relating to our health shall be treated with the greatest degree of safety and confidentiality. Both legislation and technology must be aimed at ensuring that health information on individuals is not subject to unauthorised use. The message outlines many good tools for privacy protection. Use of the right of reservation and consent for the secondary use of health information shall also be investigated. That the message has been received so positively, promises well for the further work on implementing the ICT strategy. I notice that the department of ongoing preparatory work has had broad contact with a large number of different professionals, through conferences, meetings and other venues, as well as through its own blog. Also, several private actors have either been in dialogue with the ministry or made recordings via the network. It is therefore wrong when civil society parties claim in their comments that private health sector actors are not involved in the work of the ICT Communication. Because they do, actually. On the other hand, some private potential partners may not necessarily be able to adapt to a system established by a national strategy. It's e.g. not sure that private commercial providers – owned by large international health group – can. These actors operate in a number of countries, and naturally have to deal primarily with solutions and choices made by their own group management. And this will not necessarily be consistent with Norwegian strategic choices. I understand that there may be a line in the bill for parties such as the Right and the Advance Party, who will have the right to freedom of establishment for such actors. But for the rest of us, who have looked forward to this message, this is a very happy day.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e1041	We are now processing a big message that has been given the title of One built-in – one journal. I hear that this message is being presented as a revolutionary news for the health sector and patient safety, and sees that the principle will be the subject of debate and vote at the National Labour Party meeting later this year. For me, the idea is neither new nor revolutionary. This has been a topic for several years both in the Norwegian health system and in our neighbouring countries. In Sweden, at least since 2005, they have had the job title One patient – one journal, which I see as much more precise, because it is only when one is a patient that the need for a journal occurs. In today’s society, it should also be obvious that healthcare professionals who have a patient for treatment have access to all health information about the patient, if the patient allows it. Patients should also have electronic access to everything in their own journals, in order to fill the vision of real content user participation. Unfortunately, we have not been there today, and there are many reasons for this. I note that government parties point out that the health and care sector in Norway has made significant efforts to implement information technology. It is possible that the red-greens need to draw such a picture, and one should listen to self-crust because it comes from the heart, it's called. Health services often talk about realising patients who have lost contact with reality. There is also a clear need for some representatives to be realistically informed. Norway has a fragmented healthcare system, and ICT solutions also have an impact on it. The transition from a large machine with stupid terminals to PC-based solutions and new platforms has not just gone without pain. The sector is still characterised by many proprietary solutions, random investments, lack of standardisation and low compatibility. In the wake of the Interaction Reform, the health services now needed more fax machines — or taxis — to send their records and important information on medical information and treatment needs. I'm not saying, of course, that Health Norway is a developing country in terms of technology and ICT solutions. Large amounts of money are invested each year, and ICT support has become an increasingly central part of modern healthcare. The challenge is that too little attention has been paid to the ability of the systems to communicate. Just take an example from my own home country. We have a hospital to serve more than 250,000 people – similar to a small village out in Europe. At the hospital there are 58 databases of patient information – after what I have heard. That is, the patient's personalia can actually be registered 58 times in one hospital only. The worst thing is that many of these databases cannot share data with each other, and it is hardly possible for a physician to obtain the medical information from the different databases at the same time in order to be able to see them in the context. Of course, we all want to do something about this, but the challenge is how we approach the problem. Other countries have tried national strategies and final solutions that have been tried to overcome health. It has been a failure and has cost incredible sums. The Progress Party has more faith in solutions that come from below and up. For data solutions to be successful, they need to be perceived as useful work tools that facilitate work, and not as heavy-duty, untransparent and binding. We need systems that can talk and exchange information – both vertically and horizontally in the administration. We need systems that can exchange information across different technological platforms. We need systems that can be available to all service providers, regardless of whether they are public, private or ideal. Nine out of ten people seeking health care get it in the municipality or in the hospital. The point of success in compiling journal data and coordinating computer systems so that they are adapted to form complete processing chains will be the path to which one chooses further. We know other countries that have tried and failed. The paper today outlines a vision, but it does not point to concrete measures to reach the vision. It's kind of going on in every direction. The Progress Party believes that there is now a need for a financing plan to enable the vision contained in this Communication to become a reality. This must be part of the Big One. The road choices made will be essential to the achievement of the objective. Therefore, together with the Right and Christian People's Party, we are promoting a proposal here today to ensure such political anchoring and follow-up. We believe that, and I therefore hope that the government parties will be involved in supporting the proposal that I am addressing. The left has distributed two proposals to the representatives' seats, which will probably be taken up later today. I would like to wait a little while for the explanations about these proposals to give our vote on it.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e1189	This is an important day because we are now discussing an overall message about e-health in a single big deal. That's good.
