In the Single European Act, the government sets out its objectives and outlines the direction of ICT development in healthcare. The title ‘one resident — one journal’ points to the government’s objective, namely that healthcare professionals should have access to all necessary patient information quickly and efficiently — I stress ‘necessary’ in response to Trend articles before me. To this end, we need a modern ICT platform – a common national solution that includes the whole healthcare sector. This is an extensive work in which we play on the breadth and experience of the whole sector. We're in the process of evaluating solutions, but I'm not underestimating the complexity of this problem. That's why I think we should set realistic goals and move forward step by step. In the past, most of the health care was provided in one activity. Our law is then arranged. Increased specialisation and cross-action mean that a number of establishments are currently continuously involved in the treatment and follow-up of one and the same patient. Cross-border information sharing, which ensures this reality, is not adequately addressed in today’s legislation. Therefore, the government – and we have already started – proposes a comprehensive review of the legislation, so that the record information can follow the patient and the user throughout the course of treatment. At the same time, we need to ensure good privacy – let there be no doubt about it. At least let's not construct disagreements. Information related to your and my health shall be treated with maximum safety and confidentiality. The healthcare sector has a long tradition of doing so. Health professionals are subject to strict professional secrecy and have a high level of awareness about these issues. Nevertheless, I would like to stress that privacy is more than a matter of confidentiality. We also need to ensure that personal data is used correctly. Correct and up-to-date information shall be available to the appropriate person in a timely manner. Good privacy requires us to balance these considerations in a good way. I see that it will take time to reach the goal of a common national patient record. Today's activities must not stop. We have to think in the long term and in the short term at the same time. The good work in progress on improving electronic interaction shall continue. The health network is the secure infrastructure for electronic interaction in the health and care sector. The network has seen a significant increase in both users and traffic in recent years. The number of electronic messages has increased from 130 000 per day in 2011 to 580000 per day in 2013. It's a strong development. All hospitals, all fixed doctors and many other processing groups are already connected to the network. Almost all municipalities are running. Our goal is for all municipalities to be involved in the electronic exchange of health information with hospitals and permanent doctors by 2014. So here are both dates and ambitious goals. National nuclear records shall be tested in Trondheim in the autumn of 2013, followed by Stavanger. The solution provides rapid access to critical patient information, is important for the health care of the individual patient and will simplify the everyday life of healthcare professionals. Electronic prescriptions have been used by permanent doctors and pharmacies in all municipalities in the country, and hospitals are to be included by 2015 – a new target. By changing the Health Register Act, we can now demand the use of standards for the exchange of health information. This is very important. The use of a standard shall no longer be voluntary. A regulation on standardisation is being worked on, to be sent to the hearing this spring. This is one of our most important means of achieving a coherent offer across the country. I am pleased that we have reached a political agreement on a common target. It's important for the follow-up, and it's a good starting point for further work. I record that the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Right believe that the government refuses to set ambitious targets of fear of not achieving the goals. I do not agree with that. ‘One citizen — one journal’ is perhaps one of the most ambitious public ICT surveys ever. But I believe that experience in other areas suggests that we are realistic here, that we set ourselves goals we can reach, and that we do so in a way that balances the need for legislation, standards, appropriations and equipment. If we are to succeed, we need to do a thorough preparatory work, learn from other major ICT projects and – as proposed by the Storing Communication – move step by step through regional development. We have a good starting point – the Nordics are online, and we are in the top level using electronic patient records. But we must continue, and then we need clear objectives, better coordination, up-to-date legislation and a new technological commitment. Here I believe that the staging message shows the way.

When you think it's ambitious, while all that's actually happened from 2009 to today, it's not, you have a big explanation problem and credibility problem out in the field.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e1354	In this case, a number of proposals are made for decisions aimed at preventing children from accessing tobacco products, making more arenas smoke-free and strengthening the protection against passive smoking. The proposals that are being made include: based on the Tobacco Convention, the Children’s Convention, new knowledge of health damage related to passive smoking, experience in the practice of tobacco law and experience from other countries. The legislative proposals are important elements of a larger package of measures aimed at reducing and preventing the harmful effects of tobacco use. It is emphasised that even if smoke-free tobacco is not as harmful to health as smoking tobacco, its use would also pose a risk of serious health damage. The proposals mentioned in the proposal therefore cover tobacco products in general. The World Health Organisation has established that smoking is the main risk factor for both early death and loss of healthy life years in high-income countries such as Norway. Smoking is the single most important factor for people's health, and is the lifestyle factor in which social differences are most evident. National Public Health Institute calculations from 2012 show that every year around 5,100 people die from smoking. That equals 13 pst. of all deaths in Norway. Daily smokers in Norway have been halved for the last 15 years, but the proportion of day smokers is still too high. In 2011, about 17 p.m. of the population daily. There has also been a large increase in snuff use in recent years. In 2011, 41 pp. by young men between 16 and 24 years of age daily or occasionally. The first focus area in this case concerns measures to limit young people’s access to tobacco. The plurality of the Committee will introduce a municipal authorisation scheme for the sale of tobacco. Today there is a ban on selling tobacco to people under 18 years of age. However, studies show that the age limit is too low to be enforced, and no one is currently responsible for monitoring the practice of outlets. Community aid scheme will ensure effective control and prevent the use of tobacco halls for minors. The Committee has opened up the possibility of provisional authorisation for the sale of tobacco products on a single basis and that the Ministry may lay down the criteria for this in order to ensure that tobacco policy concerns are taken into account. The Committee’s plurality will propose to ban the sale of small tobacco packages, as recommended by both the Tobacco Convention and the EU. This has already been introduced in 18 EU countries, including in Sweden, Denmark and Finland. In order to limit young people’s access to tobacco products, the Committee’s plurality will also have a general ban on the self-denomination of tobacco products. The World Health Organisation’s evaluation of tobacco work in Norway has recommended us to introduce completely non-smoking workplaces. Therefore, it is now proposed by the majority to lift the destination if the possibility of creating specific smoking rooms. The main rule shall now be that all workrooms, meeting rooms, other local and transport modes where access is generally available become totally smoke-free. An exception that is passed on is an institution's indemnity. It is also proposed to develop national guidelines for outreach activities in view of the acceptability of built-in land. Children and young people shall be assured of tobacco-free growth. The plurality of the Committee agrees with the proposal to introduce a ban on tobacco-free schooling for all elephants even to continue schooling. In addition, it is proposed to ban tobacco use in the school area, so that no tobacco can be added or used in the school. The third focus of this case on changes in the tobacco law is to improve protection against passive smoking. The majority of the Committee advocates a general destination which requires all those responsible for children to ensure that they are not exposed to passive smoking. The destination is normative and the breach of the destination is not subject to sanctions. The majority, including the incubated Right, will legislate a smoke-free entry party to health institutions and public institutions. In addition, a number of laws will have minor changes, including: are banned from tobacco sponsorship and bans on the testing of tobacco products are clarified in this case. In the setting it turns out that the Labour Party, SV, the Centre Party and the Christian People's Party are united on the amendments, while the Right Party and the Advance Party are essentially opposed. So it's a narrower political plurality for today's changes than it was when smokenova was introduced. In conclusion, I find it regrettable that the Right is moving away from the responsible public health line that the party previously led, and today there is no guarantee of a broad political plurality for important measures in tobacco prevention in Norway.

When this is done by means of a national law decision, without actually having good sanctions or controls, we are afraid that this will not give the effect we want here either. In addition to that, I would like to address the suggestions that the right-hand side promotes in the vote.

The area covered are full range requirements, Internet pharmacy, blue prescription schemes, composition of National Councils for Quality and Priorities, de minimis limits and VAT on medicinal products, and the need for research, development and innovation, and to speed up the introduction of new promising medicinal products in the specialist services. Governments have provided a thorough and comprehensive response to the different issues raised. These questions will be worked on in the Department already, they will be warned, and the Council of State also writes in the letter that no decision has yet been taken on whether these issues should be submitted to the Stortinget in its own pharmaceutical notification or in any other way. I want to say that I can welcome a big message. It has been many years since the adoption of the previous big message, and at least it is my impression – and in part my concern – that there are still debates in the media and here on the Biggest on challenges in different areas of medicine and on pharmaceutical policy. It is good to debate, and it is necessary, but it may also be a symptom that some of the important principles of pharmaceutical policy are not well enough anchored. In the last two weeks, there has been a fierce debate on public funding for the use of two new cancer drugs. On March 17th, the evening post had a leader on this as I thought there was reason to reflect. One pointed out that the health director in that debate was met with the simple sheiks. The leader writes that the case showed how easily important the principle and real dilemmas are being circumvented and that the absence of clear priorities in the field of medicinal products can lead to injustice where the profession of excellence, strong patient organisations and commercial interests are gaining ground. The leader may have something to do with it. This theme is a great news message for you. Medical policy is a priority, and we should have a thorough debate on this at Stortinget. If we receive a new notification of a medicinal product, it must show the principle of governance and not least the priority within the field of medicinal products. It is a central area of medicine, and it has a significant impact on our health supply. The previous message had three main areas: correct price, correct usage and faster access. In the debates that are held in the media and here at Stortinget, I notice that what is being focused most on is the first and the last main area, namely the right price and faster access. It is also a major focus on medicines such as industry and nutrition, and it is important, but I am concerned that we are not focusing too little on the right pharmaceutical use in the population. Today, perhaps overmedication is an equally large and perhaps larger problem than too little access to medicine. Among the elderly, we know that overmedication and mismedication are a major problem. Among hospitalised patients, mismedication is the cause of many injuries to patients and the loss of years of life. We have to focus on the fact that the most common medicines, the most commonly used, and which are basically the safe drugs, are being used properly. It's e.g. Concerned that there is a large increase in the use of paracetamol among children and adolescents. Use has doubled in 20 years. In 2003–08, 35 deaths were recorded due to incorrect use of paracetamol. In a study referred to in the Norwegian Medical Association's Journal, we find figures up to 40,000 in the Nordics with drug-induced headaches, i.e. that they get headaches because they take too much painkillers. Thus, ensuring proper use of medicine in the population and preventing over-consumption is an important area of medical policy. Then I'll show you one thing that was up in the Storting before in the spring, in the Culture Committee. The Stortinget then voted down a proposal from the Right and the Advance Party to change the broadcasting law and remove the ban on television advertising just for drug approval for hall in the store, such as paracetamol. The question of advertising for medicines I think is health policy and not cultural policy, and I look forward to discussing pharmaceutical policy in a broader context on a later occasion.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e1613	Since we're getting well warmed up here, I'll say, I'm convinced, I'm too notified. Indeed, the objectives in the field of medicinal products, as presented in the 2005 notification of medicinal products, are set. There is broad political agreement that we should have access to safe and effective medicinal products irrespective of their ability to pay, that medicinal products should be used properly professionally and economically, and that there should be the lowest possible price of medicinal products. The quality report, to which reference was made, is for treatment in Stortingen, which mentions several of the issues raised. The notifications are important for the proper use of medicinal products and provide guidance for patient safety, more systematic testing and the introduction of new treatment methods and better quality through knowledge and innovation. Let me then mention some of the points we are working on in connection with the representative proposal, as I would say – and I have responded to this in a letter – is an interesting and exciting proposal with many good considerations. The amendment to the 2008 Blue Recipe Regulation provides for a more dynamic reimbursement system. At the same time, it is necessary to require that medicinal products be effective and safe and that costs be proportionate to their effectiveness. We want to monitor expenditure on social security, and funding for medicinal products must be given priority over other measures. The de minimis limit determines who is authorised to grant a pre-authorised refund and, as we know, the State Medicines Agency may grant a pre-authorised refund if the total cost does not exceed the de minimis limit. Medicinal products that will impose additional costs on social security must be presented in the budgetary processes, as we know. This limit – the de minimis limit – is a balance between the rapid introduction of new medicinal products on pre-authorised reimbursement and the control of costs of popular protection. It is important to stress that the de minimis level may not necessarily be the reason why new medicinal products are not granted a pre-authorised refund, but that the Agency has assessed that the medicinal product does not meet the professional criteria for reimbursement, such as: the cost-effectiveness requirement, which we cannot choose from. If the professional criteria are not met, no refund is granted in advance even if the costs are below the de minimis level. There are complex reasons why drug use errors occur, and this is addressed in particular. in the main message and in the patient safety campaign in safe hands. This is about the coherence of medical lists. The aim is to prevent damage to patients as a result of a lack of medical information when the patient is admitted or discharged from a hospital. We have the right area of drug use in hospitals and in the home. The Health Directorate has provided a guide for structured drug reviews and a package of measures to promote the proper use of medicines in hospitals and in home care. Our long-term commitment to research, development and innovation in the healthcare sector also involves commitments in the pharmaceutical field. Here we have a holistic approach to coordination of the department's sectoral responsibility and the instrument. Measures to increase the scope of clinical studies and innovation and commercialisation in the field of medicinal products, to which the writer is concerned, are highlighted in several Communications to the Stortinge. The measures have produced results. After years of decline in the number of drug studies, we saw a positive increase in 2012. The increase was most marked in cancer, with almost 100 p.p., but also in public-initiated studies there was an increase of about 60 p.p. The government's commitment to biotechnology is enshrined in the National Strategy for Research and Development in Biotechnology. I want – and I am doing it now – to set up a new system for the introduction of new technologies and new medicines in the specialist healthcare service. This has been well received and will contribute to more transparent decision-making processes and a more coherent knowledge-based process. The government will facilitate increased access to trial processing. I want a clearer framework for testing and introducing new methods in the service, where one of the key instruments is to establish a set of national principles for tried and tested treatment. This shall contribute to the fulfilment of requirements of professional soundness, to the provision of information to the patient, to the consent, follow-up and monitoring of the patient and to the demonstration of the treatment effect.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e1938	The government is now finalising its proposal for a national transport plan, and in this context, it is high time to discuss which standard to use when building new roads in Norway. Over the past few weeks, we have seen a number of so-called leakages from proposals for a national transport plan. I was actually quite happy last week when we heard that the government was going to build a four-field road from Kristiansand to Lindesnes. It was a happy day. Had it been committee leader Hareide who had come with the good news, one might have thought it had come from above – when it came from the Center Party, I am a little insecure. I hope this is from the government, and I'm excited to see how to finance the expansion. Today, the State Road Service Manual and its requirements are to be used as a basis for the way the roads are to be looked. Among other things, the requirement to set up middle parts is that it should pass at least 8,000 cars per day on the journey. At the same time, the requirement for the construction of four-field roads is set at 12,000 cars per year. When roads are to be expanded and modernised, existing traffic is to be taken into account. In addition, expected traffic growth is added after 20 years. And if the road after 20 years is expected to have a traffic of more than 12,000 cars per day, it will be developed as a four-field road. But if the expected traffic is lower, the road will only be built up as a two-way road. This may be reasonable on the basis of the criteria underlying today, and if it were the case that the traffic forecasts that the Directorate of Road, the Department and the Government say the State Road Service should be based on were correct. Time and again, however, it turns out that the error was calculated heavily, and the forecasts that were taken into account were too low. As a result, many new roads are undersized from the time they are built. It is not just time and time again, but decades in decades where incorrect predictions have been taken into account. Examples are in line – E6 Eidetunnelen, which was opened in 1999. Already the year the road was opened, it turned out that the traffic figures were higher than they are to be in 2019, when the road is 20 years old. It indicates completely that this was a real record bomb. In my own home country, Telemark, it turned out that Grenlandsbrua, which was completed and opened in 1996, and which everyone in the State Road Service said was built to meet traffic growth over the next 50–60 years, had more traffic than the requirement of quadruple bridges. This means that when you are about to continue developing the E18, you have to build a new road next to the one that was built and finished through Porsgrund in 1996. We have to build a completely new border bridge, either with today's bridge or as a whole new bridge next to the existing one. Everyone understands that this is going to be much more expensive than it would have been to finish the bridge as a four-field bridge in 1996. Everyone who's here in the hall realizes that it's going to cost a lot more to build new bridges and tunnels as quadruple roads than it would have been to do it the time you built it. Then it's probably the way Assistant Road Director Lars Aksnes said to the Evening Post on April 11, 2011: «In the light of the postwar clock, one might say that we should perhaps have expanded the four-field road to a greater extent». It is very good that it is possible to admit that you have made mistakes, but then it may be time to stop a little to see if the methods we are using today, maybe have expired – and see how we are going to make sure that we do not continue to make the same mistakes over and over again. Unfortunately, it looks like you don't really want to take this into account, because it's still the same requirements that apply. The same way to calculate traffic growth is used. This happens despite the fact that calculations such as, among other things, The NHO has led Oslo Economics to perform, showing that the traffic figures on which the State Road Service provided proposed the extension of the route Search-Algård as a two-field road is much lower than the actual traffic figures. Oslo Eonomics’ traffic figures show that only in the last seven years, traffic growth has increased by 50 p.m., and the analysis suggests that four-field roads should be built throughout the route. Of course, it will mean that there won't be enough traffic on the entire route, but the E39 is the main trajectory between Kristiansand and Stavanger. I believe – and hope – that this is why the Council of State will see that these are conditions that make it necessary to develop the core road network with a standard that is very similar to the whole route. I therefore hope that during this debate the Council of State will clarify whether it will continue to make the same mistakes as we did in Norway both in the 1960s, in the 1970s and, not least, in the 1980s. In fact, we also do it in the 2000s – when we build roads with narrow road shoulders, building roads that are not dimensioned to meet future traffic growth. We only have many examples in this period and the previous one. According to former State Councillor Meltveit Kleppa, we have not managed to build as much roadway with middle parts as, for example. Sweden’s neighbouring country has done – precisely because we didn’t think as long-term as ‘sweet brother’, by having wide road shoulders and by being able to take advantage of them when it was necessary to build up roads and establish middle parts to reduce accidents. I therefore want to challenge the Council of State with some specific questions that I hope we can get answers to during this debate: – Does the Council of State think it makes sense to build new roads with scarce capacity, and is this good economy for society as a whole? – Do the Council of State agree that all parts of the core road network should have the same standard? – What has been done to make the annual forecast based more reliable? Is it the Ministry of Finance that continues to forecast expected growth, or is it perhaps more likely that the experts in the Directorate of Roads will be included when new forecasts are to be made? – How do you take into account that traffic growth is very different in different parts of the country? – What is done to make sure that you are less square when determining the standard on the road network? – Why not take more account of the rush hour traffic or traffic peaks when developing new roads? It is important to bear in mind that today’s way of calculating ÅDT, daily traffic, covers parts of the traffic load that you see on a route because it does not take into account the fact that some roads during periods have much higher traffic than is normal, for example: Trysilvegen. Trysil has a huge difference in traffic from a summer day versus when it is now soon Easter emigration, and everyone is going on the mountain or on his way to the cabin. This is also the situation that should have been taken into account when expanding new roads, actually seeing that there are different conditions than just ÅDT, which is an average of the whole year. You actually have to look at when the traffic is bigger. I want to challenge the Council of State, because in my own municipality there is a big discussion about building a four-field road with 23 meters or 20 meters of road width. All the recommendations of the NCC, which is one of the providers providing services on the road, say that it would be stupid to build 20 meters and not 23 meters because they save a lot on operating costs. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2003	First, for the sake of order, let me say that what was published in the case of KVU and KS1 for Sägne–Ålgård last week was not a leak of any kind, but rather a finished KS1 in the government, and it is to be published. And it was published. In this KS1, the government has chosen to go further with what we call the mutilation plant concept, which is based on recommendations both from the State Road Service, and also from what external quality insurers have done in terms of recommendations related to the further planning of the route. It is the road standards that underpin the choice of standard for the development of roads. Road standards are professionally justified. The standard requirements for road standards are determined on the basis of a number of factors, such as construction costs, lifetime costs, land use interventions, environment, road safety, traffic management in the normal situation and traffic management in the event of incidents and when road work is involved. In current road standards, the distinction between two-field road with pass-by and centre-carrier and four-field road is made at an ÅDT of 12000 vehicles in the forecast year, which is 20 years after the expected opening of the road. As far as road safety is concerned, the four-field road with pass-by fields and secondary works is approximately equivalent to that traffic figure, i.e. around 12000 vehicles. In the case of traffic winding-up, the road standard S5 with secondary works usually works well also at an ÅDT of more than 12000 vehicles. Some queue problems may occur when ÅDT exceeds 15 000 vehicles. Significant cooling problems first arise at an ÅDT of more than 20 000 vehicles. The current ÅDT limit for the four-field road would thus be rather robust even if traffic growth were to be slightly higher than the forecasts from the State Road Service. The cost of construction of four-field roads is significantly higher than that of two-field roads with pass-by fields and mid-driving works. This is not only due to greater road width, it is also due to the introduction of rigidity of line, wider bridges, longer cross bridges and a number of other conditions. Therefore, a lower entry point for four-field road requirements would result in significantly higher investment costs without significantly increasing the benefits. This is not a socially efficient use of means of transport. I and the government are therefore not prepared to review the professional advice and lower the entry point for the four-field road requirements. The representative Hoksrud refers to the selection of the standard on E39 on the route Search-Aelgård. The State Road Service has developed a concept selection investigation, KVU, for this route, and it is externally quality-secured through a so-called KS1. Both the State Road Service and the external quality assurance system have, based on current traffic forecasts and road standards, recommended four-field road between Search and Vigeland and two-field road with pass-by fields and mid-driving on the route further west to Ålgård. This is referred to as the so-called mid-station concept, which is then also supported by the government. Several alternative concepts were considered, including the development of four-field roads throughout the route. However, the assessment by the road authorities and external quality insurers was that the extra costs of a four-field concept were significant without correspondingly greater societal benefits. The government has discussed the matter, and, as I said, we recently concluded that further planning should be based on the midpoint concept. The amount of heavy traffic is one of the factors that will affect the extent of the pass-by field on the route towards Ålgård. On behalf of the NHO, Oslo Economics has produced a report on the success of traffic forecasts. The representative of Hoksrud seems to believe that the report states that the government has miscalculated the calculated traffic growth of E39 between Kristiansand and Stavanger. This isn't right. Oslo Economics has not produced its own traffic forecasts, nor have they assessed the professional basis of the State Road Service forecasts. On the other hand, Oslo Economics has assessed expected traffic growth compared to actual traffic growth in recent years. During this period there has been particularly high traffic growth on the E39 between Kristiansand and Stavanger. Annual growth has varied from just under 3 pst. to a little over 5 pst. If that growth were to continue over the next 25-30 years, and you made a clean projection, the traffic in the forecast year would be significantly higher than the forecasts of the State Road Service. However, based on the population projections of the SSB and on economic forecasts from the Ministry of Finance, the State Road Administration has taken on the basis of an annual traffic growth of about 1,5-2 pst. The representative of Hoksrud believes that there is already a need for a six-field road on E6 Oslo – Sweden and E39 Sandnes – Stavanger. We do not have provisions on the six-field road impact point in road normals. However, the ODD of 50000 vehicles may be used as an indicative point of impact. ODD on E6 near Moss is currently on about 35,000 vehicles. At E6 otherwise in Eastfold, the ÅDT is much lower than that. At Stavanger, the problem is a little different than in Eastfold. Towards cities, a balanced capacity should be based on the objective set out in the Climate Action Communication to take up the growth of passenger transport in the metropolitan areas of public transport, by-pass and by-pass. It is also necessary to consider what the transport system as such and the environment can withstand. Balanced capacity means that the access roads should be dimensioned according to the number of cars the system and its surroundings can withstand further towards the centre and on the basis of the objective desired for distribution between car traffic and other modes of transport. As I said, the government has chosen the concept of further planning on the route Search-Aelgård. We believe that there is a robust solution based on the forecasts of the State Road Service for ÅDT on the route. There are varying ÅDT on the route. We do not see it as possible to dimension road development in Norway from the peaks in traffic, for example. from holiday settlement or weekend exit. This would mean a significant over-dimensioning of Norwegian roads. A certain consideration may be given to it on some heavily tourist-traffic roads, but it cannot be decisive for their forecasting and forecasting. It is such that there is a great need for the field of transportation around the country. If one now chose to over-dimension some sections, and then with little social benefits in return, it would have to mean that you have to prioritize some other sections, or not extend other sections that also need to be developed. In isolation, but also overall, it will be a poor use of the total means of transport available.

Therefore, we will continue to be knowledge-based in our approach, we must do what works best first to reduce road safety risks, and we will give proper priority within the economic framework that we actually have at our disposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2418	This is a brand day. It is a great pleasure for me to present the Committee's consideration of matters for a plenary debate. I would also like to take this opportunity to thank the members of the Committee for their good cooperation on matters. Norway agrees to the ratification of the rights of disabled people. It has taken time, but time has been used to meet the requirements of the Convention. When a text of the Convention against Norwegian law began to be assessed, the law on defence was considered to be a major change in order to comply with the requirements set by the Convention to us. Fortunately, it is a unanimous committee that emphasises that ratification will help to ensure equal opportunities for people with disabilities to realise their human rights. The Committee also emphasises the need to build up barriers to participation in all the arenas of society. All human beings shall have the possibility of fulfilling their human rights and fundamental freedoms. It should be a wake-up call for all of us that there is still a need for this convention. There is solid evidence that disabled people are excluded from exercising their rights as citizens in different parts of the world, in different situations and for very different reasons. Discrimination is multifaceted: non-availability of polling facilities, not access to education, lack of accessibility to the different areas of society in leisure time, not access to ordinary working life — it is possible to continue to list. Our job to ensure the rights of people with disabilities will require continuous awareness of the theme at all levels of society. Common basic positions on non-discrimination of persons must be respected at all levels of administration and civil society. This requires information, and it requires debate. In the 2013 state budget, we have committed funds to projects that can strengthen the awareness-raising work. One of the criteria selected is inter alia: measures to contribute to the implementation of the Convention. The ratification of the Convention strengthens the work on gender equality for people with disabilities. The SSB submitted the report ‘Persons with disabilities’ earlier, last month. Indicators on living conditions and equality’, No. 8/2013. We have for some time had weak evidence of the condition. However, this makes more living conditions visible where disabled people as a group have lower counts than in the general population. There is a lower proportion of jobs, there is a lower level of education than in the general population, and there is a higher proportion of low-income households. This makes it visible that disabled people as a group do not fully participate in all the arenas of society. That's why it's with pleasure that we're submitting a housing report that requires universal design. That is why we have adopted a job strategy, and we are increasing the funds and transfers to the municipalities, so that they can carry out their tasks better – for everyone. This is gender equality in form and practice. Norway has been named one of the most equal societies in the world several times, but even here, there are some who are more like it than others. That's why we're joining the convention – the right to participate in the same line with our friend, the right to be able to be claimed on the same line with our friends. Discrimination often comes in lenient forms: there is no requirement for the individual, no effort is expected. The right to take decisions itself and the right to be given obligations and claims fail. During the hearings, questions were raised about the supplementary protocol, which I would like to comment on. The representative, Mr. Davøy, asked questions about this to the Minister for Foreign Affairs in the autumn, and I chose to include the answer to written question No. 1997. The Minister for Foreign Affairs shall reply: ‘The work of facilitating Norwegian ratification of the Convention required a comprehensive review of Norwegian legislation and practices, and this work was given priority. We will, after the Convention has been ratified, review the consequences of the ratification of the Protocol on Individual Right of Appeal before we take a decision on this issue.» Then I'll make a mistake in the text. By an incurrency, it's in the Inner East. 203 S to Prop. 106 S, page 3, column 2, third bottom paragraph: ‘The Committee therefore considers that the Convention will enter into force on 1 June 2013.’» Here it shall be “ratified” and not “in force”. With this I wish everyone in the Chamber good luck with the day. I want the rest of the population to be happy to have taken a step further towards an equal society. Then I wish the government good luck in implementing the convention.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2458	30 March this year is six years since Norway signed this convention as one of the first countries in the world. The Progress Party believes that it has taken an unnecessary amount of time before we get to where we are today, that we should ratify – but it is a pleasure that this is happening. Other laws and regulations have already been adopted to ensure that people with disabilities have equal opportunities to participate in all areas of society, but I cannot see that it has been satisfactorily followed up. On the contrary, over the last few years, we have seen that the government cuts into schemes that help people with disabilities get a better daily life. The strengthening of the rights of persons with disabilities must also be reflected in the State budgets for which the Convention is expected to be followed up. Yes, because the expectations of what a rich country can do to include everyone, are naturally big enough. Expecting the vision of a universally designed society to reach us by 2025, unless the state contributes – taking greater financial responsibility – is utopian. In the case of transport systems, they are often a prerequisite for disabled people to live an active and more social life. Existing schemes such as labour and education travel, the TT system and the social security system need to be adapted to the real needs and not to be scarce. In order for disabled persons also to be able to use public transport, there is a need for a planned upgrade with regard to the universal design of trains, trams, buses and aeroplanes, and, of course, also a planned upgrade of stations, terminals and stops. There is also a need to upgrade old public building materials and schools. Facilitation involves not only the introduction of laws and regulations that prevent discrimination but also the granting of funds for the universal design of society and for health and care measures, assistance and modern facilities. As welfare levels and life expectancy increase, a growing proportion of the population suffers from disability. Persons already disabled also live longer. Age discrimination now affects more and more older people. Older people are expected to work longer, to live in their own homes and to have an active leisure time. The Progress Party believes that this should also be possible for the elderly with disabilities and that there is reason to consider whether today’s schemes discriminate against people over 67 years of age unfairly. The need for services and organisation is individual and does not necessarily follow age. It is also a problem that disabled people over the age of 26 have to pay all training aids and training equipment themselves. This is purely age discrimination and is not justified by professional reasons. The Progress Party is strongly critical of the significant cuts made in the facility over the past few years, which has resulted in many no longer acquiring the tools from which they can benefit. The Progress Party believes that access to resources should be financed by public funding and not by local priorities, local economy or the budget framework of health enterprises. I am using the opportunity today to remind you of some of the proposals of the Progress Party that would have helped people with disabilities to be more equal than they are today. We have asked the government to present a progress plan on universal design, including the economic conditions for fulfilling the plan. We have proposals to strengthen labour-oriented measures, as far as possible participation in the labour market, proposals to introduce a state-funded and rights-based BPA scheme, proposals to introduce a service dog as a system similar to a guide for the blind, proposals to strengthen access to facilities and to improve the social security system and proposals to introduce a grant scheme for the organisation of housing for people with disabilities. The notification of housing imposes new buildings and private financing for the provision of facilities, but there are many who already live in dwellings where the state has to contribute more. Furthermore, we have proposals to have national guidelines on quality and quantity in the TT system and on a financing system that ensures TT transport in line with the guidelines. We have also proposed the removal of the 26-year rule for training and activation aids and the adoption of a stepping-up plan for adapted training and work for people with developmental disabilities. I'm going to end by saying that everything is downgraded by the government parties. This would be very good suggestions to implement to give disabled people a better everyday life. The requirements for user participation in the Convention also need to be better followed up and greater user involvement should be provided by the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman, who is now responsible for following up the Convention. Finally, I will address the proposal of the Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Advance Party. I then assume that the Labour Party and the government parties will vote in favour, if I interpreted what was said by the previous speaker, correctly.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2492	The representative of Giltun has taken up the proposal to which she referred.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2495	First, I would like to express my joy at the fact that Norway is going in today to ratify the UN Convention for People with Disabilities. It is an important milestone in the fight for the rights of people with disabilities. It is now established once and for all that the general human rights apply also to people with disabilities in Norway. Only to clear the misunderstanding: the Convention is not about special rights — on the contrary, it must guarantee disabled people the right to participate in society as equivalent citizens, with the same rights and obligations as any other citizen of society. It's high time to get this in place. But even if this is a milestone, it doesn't mean we're in the goal. With the signature and ratification of this Convention, Norway has undertaken – as it states in the Convention – to: ‘take all legislative, administrative and other measures necessary to achieve the rights conferred by this Convention’. There's still a good deal to be done here. The first – and perhaps most important – individual measure concerns universal design. Here we have agreed that Norway should be universally designed by 2025. But it's worth taking the concerns that come from disabled people's organisations, seriously: is this really fast enough? Universal design is just one aspect of many. Planning is so much more, e.g. appropriate assistance and guidance at the right moment, whether in a grocery store, in front of a mini-bank or in a polling room to perform its democratic duty. In all such situations, there is a right to the arrangements necessary for access to what is available to everyone else. It has taken a whole seven years from the convention to ratify it today – seven years – and a whole six years from we signed it until it is now up to ratification. Yes, it is true that we had to put in place the necessary legislation before the Convention could be ratified. But did it really have to take six years before the necessary law was put in place? Six years from signing to Stortinget have now finally taken a decision on the matter? I doubt that. If this was a priority problem, you could have done it earlier. I have to point out that although we are good at distributing money in this room, we are even better at distributing problems. That's why it doesn't always follow the money with the problems we distribute. For all intents: there is room for improvement and more efficient use of resources in most places, that's not what I'm saying. But when the government and the government say to an agency or a directorate, ‘Do this, in addition to what you do before’, without it going with any resources, then it's not exactly a signal that it's a priority task. That is what we are doing today: we are asking the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman to follow up and ensure that the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities is complied with – without following up with additional resources. I think that's a clear but sad signal from the government. Then there's the supplementary protocol: the government hasn't taken a position on the supplementary protocol. There may be reasons why you should go or not go to ratify it, but since the government has not taken a stand on it, we have not received the pro-arguments or the counter-arguments. To this end, the Right, together with the Advance Party and the Christian People's Party, promotes the proposal made by Mr Giltun's representative: ‘The Government is asked to carry out during the spring of 2013 an investigation that makes benefits and disadvantages visible by ratifying the Additional Protocol on the right of appeal to the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.’; When this proposal was promoted in the Committee on the Right, we were sure that it would be supported by the entire political spectrum. The reason is that just a few months ago – November 11, 2012 – a proposal with virtually exactly the same wording received support from all the parties on Stortinget. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2783	The majority is currently examining the conclusion of the Nordic Convention on Social Security of 12 June 2012. The current Nordic Convention on Social Security of 18 August 2003 adapts the social security coordination between the Nordic countries to the Community rules in the field by means of EC Regulations Nos. 1408/71 and Nos 574/72. Indirectly, the Convention has been adapted to EC Regulation No. 859/2003, which gives effect to coordination regulations for nationals from non-EU countries, so-called third-country nationals. This Regulation was not applicable to Iceland, Liechtenstein and Norway. It also does not apply to Denmark, because of a Danish reservation regarding legal cooperation under the EU Treaty. However, the Nordic Convention applies the social security regulations to all who have been social security members in the Nordic countries, including third-country nationals. In view of this, a new Nordic Convention on Social Security was drawn up and signed on 12 June 2012. The new Convention has been adapted to Regulation 883/2004 as inter alia: has an extended personality, including persons who have not been employed or self-employed. The new Convention mainly continues the main features of the previous coordination rules in the EEA and the Nordic countries. The Convention also lays down rules on the treatment of international cases in difficult areas such as family benefits where conditions are met in different countries. The increased emphasis on administrative cooperation and clearer tasks in the area of rehabilitation in cases where another country is competent may lead to some additional work and expenditure. At the same time, the new rules on dealing with internal issues are expected to bring administrative benefits. In addition, more effective rehabilitation can provide savings on the social security budgets by speeding up the return to work. It is therefore assumed that the Convention may be implemented within the applicable budgetary framework. The new Convention gives Nordic social security cooperation a clearer and stronger anchor than in the previous Conventions. It facilitates the reduction and mitigation of border barriers. The new Convention provides for the adaptation of the inter-ordinary social security coordination to the changes resulting from the new social security regulations, which are already applicable in the EU and which will apply from 1 June 2012 to the EFTA countries Iceland, Liechtenstein and Norway when they are incorporated into the EEA Agreement. The new Convention, like the 2003 Convention, is of great importance for Norway, Iceland and Denmark with regard to third-country nationals, because only the Convention provides the basis for applying the Regulation to that group in international matters. The Convention is necessary for Greenland and the Faeroe Islands to participate in the Nordic social security coordination on the same basis as the other Nordic countries. The Committee considers that the proposal for a new Convention is broadly based on the previous coordination rules for the EEA and the Nordic region. Increased labour migration poses major challenges for the coherence of legislation and responsibilities. Nav International has had major challenges with residues, but through 2012, the department has achieved significant performance improvements. The Committee believes that it is important to continue to work with a targeted approach to reducing residues and improving service to users. The Committee considers that ICT investment is expected to further streamline the work of Nav International. There is every reason to expect an increase in labour migration in the coming years. The combination of good economic growth in Norway and high unemployment in Europe contributes to this. The majority of the Committee, with the exception of the members of the Progress Party, points to the need for good coordination legislation at European and Nordic level in order to ensure that people who have acquired the right to benefits are paid correctly and promptly. The majority emphasise that this is important in order to facilitate mobility in the labour market, both in the interests of foreign workers coming to Norway and that more Norwegians are going out to work. As a resident in Eastfold, I often meet cross-border workers to work. Many people report that the legislation is difficult to obtain. My hope is that the Convention can help to make this easier and that work on it must continue. The majority consider the Convention to be an expression of a strong relationship which marked Nordic cooperation. The Committee of the Regions has also delivered an opinion on the position of the Labour and Social Committee and is in agreement with it. I recommend with the attitude of this committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e2840	The size treats today Prop. 7 L on changes in the Swedish Social Security Act to combat social security abuse. We can be proud of how we have built up a popular peace that gives security when you have lost your job, are unable to work for health reasons or can support families with children. Today's popular fear is the most important protection against poverty. Rights are acquired by taking part in the labour market. It provides more equality and individual freedom than in the countries where the individual is more dependent on family care. In line with tax evasion, social security abuse is theft of the community. Abuse undermines the willingness to fund our common security. Norwegian and foreign studies in this area expect significant dark figures for abuse of welfare schemes. In recent years, organised social security abuse has been identified involving cooperation between a large number of people and the systematic exploitation of the rules. The methods of social security fraud are becoming increasingly creative and are facing major challenges. Labour immigration and the possibilities of exporting social security benefits to other countries pose additional challenges for the control of payments from Norwegian welfare schemes. The government takes social security abuse seriously. In 2009, we were given access to several legal changes – such as the exchange of information with insurance companies in the event of suspicion of social security abuse or attempted abuse, access to information from anyone who is presumed to be able to clarify or confirm a suspicion of abuse, and access to control visits at workplaces in particularly vulnerable industries. This is also important in supporting serious work in competition with the unprofessional. In addition, amendments were introduced to the rules on recovery in order to clarify and streamline access to recover mispaid benefits. In October 2012, the amount limit for non-recovery was increased from half of the legal fee, which is DKK 430, to four times the legal fee. In recent years, the government has strengthened the control activities of the annual budgets. The Employment and Welfare Act currently uses 120 years of work on social security abuse, and has a budget for Nav control of about 200 000 people. Eighty-five million. kr. This results in more abuse being detected, reported and prosecuted. I Prop. 7 L is provided with more tools in the fight against social security abuse, such as access to information directly from the accounting officer, the obligation to legitimise, the interconnection of registers and the obligation to report to other public authorities. Access to information directly from the accounting officer may reduce the processing time when self-employed persons apply for income-dependent benefits. The establishment will thus have the same access and ability to verify the income data of traders that are available to employees, where the control point is the employer. The Committee is in favour of increased interconnection of registers. It provides re-use of data already collected, and the form of control provides fewer cases and fewer personal data that need to be reviewed by physical case handlers. The Committee agrees with the Ministry’s clarification that health or other personal data considered sensitive by the Data Protection Act should be excluded. The Committee is also concerned that those who are subject to further follow-up after verification and interconnection of registers must be notified and given a real opportunity to make their views known. Furthermore, it is important to provide an individual with insight into how data is stored and who has access to them. Decisions by other public bodies have in certain cases an impact on the right to benefits at the seat of employment and welfare. In particular, this Decision applies to the non-resident authorities, which may be of crucial importance for the membership of the population. It is proposed in the proposal that routine notifications from the foreign authorities to the State of Employment and Welfare may be provided. However, it will only be possible to give the regulation in a few minutes, as it is not technically possible to implement it today. The Committee welcomes this work. Over a number of years, the State of Employment and Welfare has, among others: in cooperation with other control establishments, identified a number of cases in which persons have received benefits by allowing other persons to act in their name against the relevant public authorities and health professionals. The Public Security Act does not currently contain any rules that explicitly require that the person promoting the requirement for performance be legitimised. Nor does the law contain any direct requirement that the person examined by health professionals with a view to obtaining a declaration on the seat of employment and welfare should be legitimised at the time of examination. Many of the abuse cases that have been identified could have been revealed by a simple identity check at the different stages of the case. In the light of this, the Committee supports the proposal. It is important that an obligation to legitimise does not stand in the way of necessary health care. Neither is the government's suggestion to read. The Progress Party shall promote proposals for quarantine. The majority of the Committee, from the Workers' Party, the SV and the Centre, do not wish a general introduction of quarantine time. Severe abuse of social security means that benefits have been provided for which they have not fulfilled the conditions. The benefits are then to be stopped and refunded. The majority also suggest that gross social security fraud also provides punishment according to other parts of the law, and that this has a good preventative effect. The majority believe that whether or not the terms of the different benefits are met should determine whether or not the benefit in question is valid. The proposal is therefore not appropriate. We take social security abuse seriously, and provide this basis for even more people to be taken. Still, I think it's important that we don't create the impression that this is a bigger problem than it actually is. You have to be careful to mix social security abuse and mispayments. Mispayments may be due to relationships between the applicant and Nav itself. We must recognise that in several areas our legislation is difficult for many. An example of this is parental leave. The Minister for Children has taken the initiative to put in place a simpler regulation. Exaggeration of the extent of social abuse can also undermine people’s confidence in welfare systems and contribute to stigmatisation of Nav’s users. Lenin once said: Trust is good, control is better. I think we can state that we never get where there will be no social security abuse. In that case, it will mean a control regime that does not meet people with confidence. When we are vulnerable and need community help, it is perhaps because it is most important to be met with trust. The Government's proposal balances well with the Community's need for control of payments and privacy considerations. I recommend with this committee's attitude and the majority's proposal.

The disclosure of social security abuse must be balanced against another important consideration: privacy. This applies in any situation where abuse is to be detected. When the police do it, when Nav does it, and when other agencies do it, there's a balance between how much one should know about a person and how that information should be managed. There have been very large and loud discussions about privacy in this room, with many more audiences present, in matters that may have been more fundamentally important. Nevertheless, it is worth noting to all data subjects that the major threat to privacy is not necessarily the major legislative proposals, but that in every legislative proposal there are technologies, coordination and opportunities that can also be at the expense of privacy. Therefore, it is essential to weigh these considerations against each other properly. In this context, the most problematic issue here is the interconnection of registers, which means that information is collected from different registers and is routinely linked – automatically – to detect abuse. There are positive aspects to it: it means, for example, to a lesser extent, there are physical case handlers who go through the information, that there are simply fewer decent people who get access to that information, because one does it automatically. At the same time, it is problematic in view of some of the fundamental pillars of privacy considerations, one of which is that information collected for one purpose requires explicit consent to be used for another. Another is to operate as far as possible with the consent of the person for whom the information has been collected. It is also an important principle to be able to correct information about itself stored by the public. All of this, of course, is about citizens' legal certainty. It is also an important point, as set out in the European Convention on Human Rights, that there should be proportionality between the intervention proposed and the concern to be taken into account. In all these areas, the government's legislative proposals have improved. Now you can choose - it's getting closer to choice - if you want a positive or a negative angle of incidence. I think I should allow myself a positive approach, even if it is election year this year: it is very positive that the government has chosen to listen to many of the hearing bodies, especially the Data Protection Authority, who have pointed out that the original bill had some key shortcomings when it comes to privacy, for example. and – perhaps most importantly – that it was too effluent and unclear what one actually had the opportunity to ask for and collide with information. The data protection authority concluded that the legislative proposal as it existed when it was sent to the hearing was outside what could be considered as approved within the EMK 8 – i.e. it was ‘disproportionate’. But the Data Protection Authority's assessment is that after the changes and tightenings that the government has made, it is within. It's positive. In general policy, there is reason to warn against always moving at the outer edge of what is permitted by privacy policy, but it is better to move at the outer edge, inside what is likely to be allowed, than to move outside. It is also very positive, once we agree on many of the ways in which social security users are treated, that a unanimous committee in many areas also addresses different privacy considerations. I am also very pleased that a joint committee – except the Progress Party, as far as I registered – is in favour of a proposal that says that you have to be explicit in legislation and regulations on what kind of information you can extract. It may sound like a trifle, perhaps, but it is a crucial principle that explicit approval is required from the legislator who can be verified when doing first things that are problematic for privacy. At the same time, the Right, together with the Christian People's Party and probably also other parties, believes that there may still be privacy challenges in the Nav system in view of consent, as far as possible. Everyone understands that it is not possible that the conclusion of the public will be that you will not get your social security if you answer no questions about whether you believe that this information about you is to be collected in order to provide you with social security – so there are some practical considerations here. Both in terms of consent, the right to correct information and some such things, there are still challenges within the Nav system. It is also worth remembering the very strong criticism of Nav’s control activities in a report from the Data Protection Authority in May 2012. Last night I received an e-mail from a user who claimed – this has not been verified – that he had a copy of a Nav report with a post IT note with assessments of his case attached to him. It is clear that there is an anecdote, but nevertheless it can illustrate some basic privacy issues in Nav: Who has access to the information? How does it float around? Who controls who has access to the information? And what is the possibility for the user – the citizen – to gain insight and to have the impressions that might be wrong? That is why the Right Party and the Christian People's Party, having supported many of the government's measures against social security fraud, have accepted a proposal asking the government to come back to the Stortinge in an appropriate way – and that is exactly what the government likes: that we don't impose too much on it – with a review of privacy and how privacy is sought in the Nav system.
