ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e3119	The issue we are going to deal with today is about older people in the workplace, and how we use the labour force and the joy of work that we have in the 60s and 70s. It's a pleasure for me – if I don't want to call myself an older employee, I have passed 60 – to be the case rapporteur in this case, especially when it gets such a solid outcome as it now seems. But I'll get back to that. Specifically, the original proposal on the so-called 70-year limit in the Working Environment Act is about raising it to 75 years. It's high time we started this discussion. I would therefore like to extend a great deal of thanks to the proponents of the Progress Party who got this discussion on the track – again. In 2011, we were introduced a new pension scheme and, in my opinion, a very good system. With this system, we no longer have a fixed retirement age, on the contrary, we have a flexible retirement system between 62 and 75 years old, and where you earn pension entitlements up to the age of 75. Many people make use of the flexibility that has been provided, for example. sees that so many people take advantage of the possibility to withdraw a pension, but still work alongside it. But parts of the law – the parts we are going to talk about today – have not followed. This applies not only to the 70-year limit of the Employment Environment Act, but also to the limits of the Civil Service Act and all the special age limits spread throughout the legislation, as well as to the occupational age limits of the Supreme Court. So older workers can't always combine social security and work as they might wish. If you're a caregiver, you have a special age limit of 65 years. You work in some private businesses, such as Mutually, you have to relate to a mandatory limit of 67 years. And even though your company does not have such an internal business frontier, the Working Environment Act says that the border is at 70 years. The Civil Service Act has a similar limit. Faced with the pension reform, we can say one thing about today's legislation: this is not related. At the same time, we know that there are some good and some less good counter-arguments against changing today's law. Everything is not black-and-white or red-blue here in the world. For example, neither the Civil Service Act nor the Health and Safety at Work Act require that an employment relationship be terminated at the age of 70. If employers want an employee who reaches the age of 70 to continue, they are free to do so. And of course, there may be cases where an older worker does not maintain the same productivity as other employees. Special rules for older workers in the law, such as the right to an additional holiday week and the right to a reduced position and more leave, can help to make older workers more expensive than those in other ages. Such things can be a challenge for small businesses with small margins. It can also affect older workers themselves by making it more difficult for them to get a new job of 50 plus in the labour market, and we know that it is difficult enough for this group already to get a new job. These are important and relevant objections that we as legislators must take into account when dealing with such a case. Such objections should not be raised, particularly when they include: come from the social partners. They are always worth listening to in such questions. But that doesn't mean we can stand still in this case and let things stay just like before. As mentioned above, current borders are not linked to the new pension reform. You simply can't adjust your life-style pensions while refusing to compensate people for it by working any longer. Moreover, as the wave of old age arrives, we cannot afford to throw out experienced, competent and motivated labour. And not least, the 70-year-old limit was fully restored in 1917. It was another time. But perhaps the most important argument for changing the law is the normative effect of the law. It is true, as the opponents of changing the law often object, that today's laws open up the possibility of continuing to work if both the employer and the employee so wish. What you forget then is the very strong signals it sends to have such a limit. What the law says in practice at the 70s is that everyone, or most of them, is not usable in the workplace after a certain date, and that's not true. As former Swedish National Council of Tora Aasland of 69 years, it said in October to Stavanger Aftenblad: ‘Standing more of us are more than competent enough to continue even if we pass 70 years.’ And signals about something else should not be sent by the law. It is not just Tora Aasland who has understood the value of older people in work, and we have recently seen a growing cross-political consensus on the issue of age limits. The Right Party and the Progress Party entered in 2010 to raise the age limit in the Working Environment Act. Now we see that the Christian People's Party has proposed the same thing in its program design. And that's not just in the opposition we see a development. Last year, Minister of Labour Hanne Bjurstrøm wrote that raising the age limit can lead to negative attitudes towards older people in general working life. From today's business no later than today, we see a labour minister with a more moderate and changeable tone. We also see that there is a proposal to raise the 70-year limit at the National Labour Party meeting. This movement is reflected in the setting on which the Storing will take a decision today. This is an idea whose time has come. Age limits are going to be raised or removed, it's essentially a question of how. There is a cross-political desire for consistency between the age limits in the law; there is no doubt about it. What is now the challenge is to do this in the right way, to take over and meet the important objections that have arisen, as well as to be able to separate the important objections from the less important ones. That is why the Right has agreed to make a compromise with the government parties and the Christian People's Party in this matter. We will therefore ask the government today to investigate this matter in consultation with the social partners, and to come back to the Stortinget with a comprehensive investigation during the spring of 2014. Such an investigation must address the field in its full scope — not only the 70-year limits of the Working Environment Act and the Civil Service Act, but also the special age limits and the opportunity to set occupational age limits. Finally, let me underline: the right supports the intention of the proposal of the Progress Party. And once again, let me thank the proposers for getting this matter up on the agenda. But rather than mechanically increase the limit in one law now, we want to address a whole issue that addresses the whole age limit issue in its full scope, one that meets the objections of employers and the rest of the world of work. This we think is the way to do it. I am convinced that the majority here today will reach a very good decision and recommend the vote.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-19.ana.ud419e3156	A broad majority of the Stortinget, all parties except the Advance Party, asks the government to assess the consequences of an increase or removal of the legal age limit of 70 years in Norwegian working life. The government is given a deadline to make such an assessment in spring 2014. The minority, who have also promoted the proposal for a representative we are discussing today, does not see the need for such an impact assessment. This is typical of this party's understanding of Norwegian labour policy. They believe and believe that today's 70th anniversary limit can be raised to 75 years with a fingertip and without the social partners being included on advice. But let's not dwell on it. I note that a large majority in the United Kingdom now believe that time is needed to raise the legal age limit, so that there is consistency between the good intentions of the pension reform and the work environment law. The purpose of the pension reform, which, in my view, is the most important freedom of choice reform we have implemented since the turn of the century, is precisely to encourage older workers to work longer because many want to do so, and because employment needs their experience, competence and workforce. We then find that the current age limit, both the legal age limit and the different age limits set by each enterprise, is counterproductive to the purpose of the pension reform. So I'm going to hurry up and say that today's debate on age limits is really a debate on dismissal protection. So, while a retirement justified by age is considered to be inconclusive before the age of 70, the employment environment law states that it is the case after the age of 70. The Labour Environment Act has rules of discrimination, including with regard to age. The provisions take the view that age in itself is not a cause for renunciation. Termination as a result of age is by definition discriminatory. Nevertheless, at the age of 70, discrimination will cease. So now we've lived with the 70s since 1917. It is incorporated and – I would say – accepted. We have also lived with and accepted the law on age limits for public officials, a law that, in my opinion, is age discrimination. Public servants and women are required to retire at the latest by the end of the month after the age of 70. While the Labour Environment Act opens up the possibility for employees and employers to work beyond 70 years, public servants and women meet a bastant demand to leave the labour market at this time. I allow myself to believe that such discrimination is not only contrary to Norwegian law – it is also contrary to EU law. The pension reform and the fact that we all live longer is the direct reason why we are now asking the government to raise the age limit question. Life expectancy is increasing on average by two months each year. We already see that more and more people work longer, even though the very few break the 70th anniversary limit. However, the fact that employees can raise retirement, work and earn new pension points to 75 years of age means that more and more people will do so when it is opened up. In a situation of minimal unemployment and high labour demand, older workers represent an important labour resource. But I also see, of course, that there are arguments for keeping the 70-year limit – both from the employer and the employee’s side counts the consideration for a decent departure. Unfairly, difficult situations may arise when employers have to cancel older workers because they are unable to do a satisfactory job if we remove the age limit. But it can slowly be claimed, completely independent of age. Although I tend to believe that people’s working capacity is not primarily dependent on age, I see, of course, that there are professions or industries with higher health and safety risks than other professions, and that it may be appropriate to maintain a compulsory retirement age. But in my view, age alone is an unacceptable ground for dismissal. However, I understand that we cannot have a statutory and contractual work where it is entirely left to the individual employee to decide when to leave the labour market. That's why this question is so complicated. It reminds me of a story from Trondheim, a little back in time, when one heroic famous newspaper editor one day met one of the longest-departed rapporteurs on the city's walkway North Gate. The editor, who had a significant share in his own newspaper, kept it going far beyond the age limit, and it was those who thought that his editorials were probably not adapted to the present. The conversation was conducted on age and age limits, and the editor was asked if he did not soon intend to drop his editor's pen. I don't do that until I start writing nonsense, the editor answered. And who is it that evaluates it, answered the follow-up question. That's what my wife, the editor found out after a pause of thought. The woman was something older than himself. The point, of course, is that we don't always have enough self-intelligence to understand when it's time to give up. Some countries have left the age limit issue to the social partners. The social partners also have a strong influence on the legislation in force in the Norwegian working life. By the way, it was supposed to be missing. On the one hand, age limits are also attached to collective agreements, and we have company age limits — usually also as a result of agreements between employers and employees. I would therefore like to stress that for the Workers' Party and – I believe – the broad majority in this Chamber, it is quite unthinkable to raise the legal age limit in Norwegian working life without the social partners being included on advice and listening to, before the next question is raised by the Stortinget. Therefore, in this context, I should be careful to provide guidance for the discussions to take place, except to indicate that there is a clear desire of the Biggest that there must be consistency between age limits and pension reform, so that everyone who wants to do so has real access to the potential of the pension reform.

These are very important arguments.’ A large part of the pension reform, the principles of the pension reform, which the Progress Party disagreed with, was that you could combine pensions and work after 62 years, where you could decide for yourself when to retire. You could make your own independent, free choices if you were 62, 63, 67, 70, or 72. At the same time, this room encourages every day: we need labour, and older people need to be used — and that resource must be used in the workplace. That's why we've also created a pension scheme that allows you to earn a pension until you're 75 years old. But you still choose to wait to decide if it's important, and if it's right that they get that opportunity, because today you choose to continue to maintain the rule that allows you to kick people at the age of 70. I don't understand that logic either. I think it is important to note one of the quotes in today's Business that comes from the Minister of Labour, which at least gives me a clear picture that there is a lot of rhetoric going on in this case, and that there are no promises that you have changed your course and that you want to sign this out. What you're actually saying is you're going to cut a sample. On the question from the journalist, the answer is: ‘Huitfeldt does not mean what the outcome of an investigation is. Employers are supposed to hire people, and they must be willing to hire seniors.’ In the quotation earlier, the Council of State said that she was willing to listen to exactly the same employers, but the good and important arguments of theirs that said that the problem if we removed this 70-year rule was that they did not have the opportunity to cancel people by hiring them, so a logical flaw is then to those degrees. It reminds me that this matter can really be summed up with the old saying that when you don't want something to happen, you set up a committee. I think it's a shame, I think it's sad that that's what's the starting point, and that's what's the actual relationship in this debate. We've had this case so many times. I respect the need for rounds. What the Progress Party is asking for is a change in the law. If our proposal had been adopted today, the parties – as we have an ongoing salary settlement – could have been invited to it tonight to discuss it in the wage settlement now in ours. Then I agree that it will be important and proper to also look at the special age limit and to look at all the age limits in other types of legislation. It has also been said by the government that it is necessary to get back in the context of the pension reform, without having received any feedback or signals about when that kind of issue comes. But it's a completely different matter from the age limit, the right that employers should have in the Environmental Protection Act to be able to cancel employees at the age of 70. I think we should be so honest and honest with each other that we keep the two things separate, but also agree that we need cleanups on both sides of that limit. So I'm sorry to say that you use the same rhetoric that you've used before; you have to consider without anything happening. You have to take it with the parties. There have been many wage settlements since we first promoted this proposal in 2006, and there has been a reasonable opportunity to bring it with the parties.
